Analysis","url":"/security-labs/threat-command/category/malware-analysis","locale":"en-us","created_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","updated_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","created_at":"2026-08-12T12:28:35.178Z","updated_at":"2026-08-14T14:34:54.590Z","ACL":{},"_version":2,"tags":[],"seo":{"canonical_tag":"","do_not_index":false,"nofollow":false,"seo_description_l10n":"","seo_image":null,"seo_title_l10n":""},"title_l10n":"Malware Analysis","publish_details":{"time":"2026-08-25T08:19:17.315Z","user":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","environment":"blt835e2c3b7e9eb7fb","locale":"en-us"}}],"created_at":"2026-08-12T13:49:54.944Z","created_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","date":"2026-05-22T00:00:00.000Z","main_content":{"markdown_l10n":"Elastic Security Labs's prior coverage of [REF6598](https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/phantom-in-the-vault) documented an intrusion set whose Windows toolchain landed via Obsidian plugin abuse, escalated via an in-memory PE loader (PHANTOMPULL), and finished with a RAT (PHANTOMPULSE). That post focused on delivery. This post analyzes the final stage: PHANTOMPULSE, an implant that ships three process-injection techniques, resolves its C2 through Ethereum/Base/Optimism transaction inputs, and bypasses UAC via the public `schuac` technique. The analysis surfaces a sinkhole-able blockchain C2 channel, a unified hardware-breakpoint primitive that disables AMSI / WLDP / ETW and pervasive AI-assisted-development fingerprints in the implant's debug strings.\n\n## Key takeaways\n\n* PHANTOMPULSE implements three injection techniques adapted from recent public offensive-security PoCs.\n* AMSI, WLDP, and ETW are bypassed via a single shared HWBP primitive  \n* The blockchain C2 resolver has **no sender verification**, allowing a defender to override the C2 URL for every implant by posting a single transaction  \n* Strong AI-assisted-development indicators present in the binary\n\n## A note on AI-assisted development\n\nPHANTOMPULSE bears strong fingerprints of AI coding assistance, visible throughout the debug strings.\n\nThe clearest tells:\n\n* **Structured step numbering** in operational logs: `[STEP 1] Staged mode — payload downloaded from C2 at runtime`, `[STEP 1/3] Scheduled Task (DotNetSvcUpdateTask, logon + every 3 min)`, `[STEP 2/3] Boot Task (DotNetSvcCoreTask, INTERACTIVE_TOKEN + BootTrigger)`, `[UNINSTALL 4/6] Removing persist_loader DLL + registry PE data...`, `[REPAIR] Reinstalling boot task (INTERACTIVE_TOKEN)...`.  \n* **ENTER/DONE function tracing**: `\"[HEIS] encrypt_text_only ENTER\"` / `\"[HEIS] encrypt_text_only DONE\"`, `\"KeylogResolveAPIs: ENTER\"`. The diagnostic style LLMs default to when generating new functions.  \n* **Verbose diagnostics**: `\"FindHostProcessEx: scan stats: total=%lu sessSkip=%lu openFail=%lu native=%lu wow64=%lu mapReject=%lu dbgReject=%lu sess=%lu\"`, `\"ManualMap: thread hijacked and resumed — DLL injection via thread hijack complete\"`. Self-explanatory output, unusually talkative for malware.  \n* **Em dashes in C strings**: `\"elevate: FAIL — no deployed DLL path\"`, `\"\u003e\u003e\u003e .elevate: NOT proxy — spawning trusted host to handle elevation\"`.\n\n![AI generated strings in the binary](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt29d8e4422794b827/6a7c79b99f5251faa1663f3a/image26.png \"AI generated strings in the binary\")\n\n## Execution chain\n\n`MainEntryLogic` is the orchestration function that runs the full initialization sequence before entering the C2 loop:\n\n```text\nstart\n └─ WinMain\n     └─ MainEntryLogic\n         1. DynInit                // Bootstrap API resolution\n         2. ElevationStateCheck    // \".elevate\" marker detection, routes by token elevation state\n         3. SingleInstanceCheck    // XOR-decrypted mutex, exit if already running\n         4. EvasionInit            // Direct syscalls + ETW HWBP\n         5. SyscallResolverInit    // CPUID + hash-based kernel32 resolution\n         6. SleepMaskInit          // Sleep obfuscation setup\n         7. ComputeMachineID       // DJB2(module name) ^ volume serial\n         8. IsRunningHollowed      // Process hollowing self-check\n         9. CollectSysInfo         // CPU, GPU, RAM, OS, AV, apps\n        10. FilelessPersist        // Drop stub DLL, registry artifact\n        11. InstallPersistence     // Three scheduled tasks via COM ITaskService\n        12. C2Loop_Init → C2Loop_Main\n```\n\nAt startup, the implant DJB2-hashes the user name and computer name and looks each up in a precomputed table. A match exits the process. Brute-forcing the table against public anti-sandbox wordlists recovered 20 of the 61 entries: `WDAGUtilityAccount` (Windows Defender Application Guard), several `DESKTOP-XXXXXXX` default-VM names, and the [Joe Sandbox personas](https://gist.github.com/0x-Apollyon/04b30400b51136a19c739a8ec4dc95d5) (`abby`, `patex`, `george`, `john`, `lisa`, `frank`, `RDhJ0CNFevzX`).\n\n## Defense evasion\n\n### Direct syscalls and API wrappers\n\nPHANTOMPULSE resolves ntdll functions by walking `PEB→Ldr` with DJB2 hashes, extracts System Service Numbers (SSNs) from each NT function's prologue, and builds private syscall stubs. These stubs are wrapped in higher-level helpers used throughout the rest of the implant:\n\n* `NtCreateFile_Wrap`  \n* `NtWriteFile_Wrap`  \n* `NtClose_Wrap`  \n* `NtCreateSection_Wrap`  \n* `NtMapViewOfSection_Wrap`  \n* `NtProtectVirtualMemory_Wrap`  \n* `NtWriteVirtualMemory_Wrap`\n\nThe rest of the implant calls these wrappers instead of `kernel32`/`ntdll` exports, defeating user-mode `ntdll` hooks (IAT replacements, inline detours, or trampoline patches) that EDR products inject into the documented API surface.\n\nA single helper function routes every disk write through `NtCreateFile` \\+ `NtWriteFile` directly, with delete-and-retry on access errors.\n\n### String and config obfuscation\n\nPHANTOMPULSE uses four XOR layers for different artifacts:\n\n| What | Key | Where the key lives |\n| ----- | ----- | ----- |\n| C2 fallback URL, mutex, drop-path filenames | 16-byte: `F7 7C 8E 40 DF C1 7B E5 E7 4D 86 79 D5 B3 53 41` | Embedded in `.rdata` |\n| Blockchain provider hostnames (UTF-16 LE) | 8-byte: `5A 3C 7E 1D 9F 2B 4E 8A` | Embedded in `.rdata` |\n| COM Elevation Moniker, keylog file payload | `0xE95CA237`, computed at runtime to keep the constant out of `.rdata` | Computed, not stored |\n| C2 URL pulled from blockchain transaction `input` | The resolver wallet address itself | Reused from the public lookup key |\n\n![String dexoring stub](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt0095e3fcd07551e0/6a7c79bc96b5a6e3a4875470/image13.png \"String dexoring stub\")\n\n### AMSI, WLDP, and ETW bypass via hardware breakpoints\n\nPHANTOMPULSE disables AMSI, the Windows Lockdown Policy code-trust check, and ETW telemetry through a single shared primitive: a hardware breakpoint planted on each API entry, intercepted by a vectored exception handler that fakes the return value without inline patching.\n\n| Slot | Target API | Spoofed return (RAX) |\n| ----- | ----- | ----- |\n| DR0 | `WldpQueryDynamicCodeTrust` | `0` (`S_OK`) |\n| DR1 | `AmsiScanBuffer` | `0x80070057` (`E_INVALIDARG`) |\n| DR2 | `EtwEventWrite` | `0` (`STATUS_SUCCESS`) |\n\n![Hardware breakpoint setup function](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt7d87d620122ec033/6a7c79bfbdcff027fcc3cfab/image22.png \"Hardware breakpoint setup function\")\n\nThe mechanism, step by step:\n\n1. The implant resolves the target API. AMSI and WLDP go through `LoadLibraryA` \\+ hash-based export lookup; ETW uses a PEB→Ldr walk since ntdll is already loaded.  \n2. The HWBP descriptor (target API address, mode, spoofed return value) is written into one of four 40-byte slots in a global slot table.\n3. A helper thread suspends the target thread, calls `NtGetContextThread` / `NtSetContextThread` to write DR0–DR3 \\+ DR7, then resumes. (If the implant's vectored exception handler is already installed, an in-process `STATUS_BREAKPOINT` is raised instead, letting the VEH read the slot table and program the DRs without a helper thread.)  \n4. When the protected API is called, the CPU raises `Debug Exception` on the function's first instruction.  \n5. The implant's vectored exception handler intercepts the `Debug Exception`, walks its 4-slot table to find the firing address, and modifies the thread context: `CONTEXT.Rax` is set to the per-slot spoofed return value, `CONTEXT.Rip` is redirected to a pre-stored \"skip\" thunk that returns to the caller.  \n6. The handler returns `EXCEPTION_CONTINUE_EXECUTION`. The caller sees the spoofed RAX as if the API had run.\n\n![Exception handler: BREAKPOINT STATUS](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt8a575368304959c4/6a7c79c1da3d052349633c56/image11.png \"Exception handler: BREAKPOINT STATUS\")\n\n\nThe dispatcher serves two paths. One handler (`VEH_Dispatcher`) processes both the implant's own `RaiseException(STATUS_BREAKPOINT)` calls (used to seed and re-program the DR registers from the slot table) and the `STATUS_SINGLE_STEP` exceptions that fire when a protected API is called. The exception code drives the branch: `STATUS_BREAKPOINT` triggers DR programming, `STATUS_SINGLE_STEP` triggers the spoof.\n\nThe handler is also not registered directly. `AddVectoredExceptionHandler` receives a tiny JMP thunk allocated at runtime in a fresh `MEM_PRIVATE` page (`VirtualAlloc` \\+ `VirtualProtect` to `PAGE_EXECUTE_READ`). The thunk is a `JMP [RIP-relative]` indirect jump (6-byte opcode `FF 25 00 00 00 00`) followed inline by the dispatcher's address. Because no bytes are ever written to `AmsiScanBuffer`, `WldpQueryDynamicCodeTrust`, or `EtwEventWrite`, signature-based detection that scans for prologue patches misses this entirely. \n\n### Build variant: active and dormant subsystems\n\nSeveral subsystems exist in the binary as code or strings, but are not active in this build. This is a stripped-down build of a larger codebase.\n\n* **NTDLL unhooking**: Debug strings for an unhooking subsystem live in `.rdata` (`UnhookNtdll: ntdll base = %p`, `applied %d relocation fixups to .text`), but nothing references them. Dead in this variant.  \n* **Registry-resident PE blob loader**: earlier builds stored the next-stage PE inside the registry. This build does not, but the uninstall routine still cleans up the legacy registry blob.  \n* **COM hijack persistence**: never installed by this build. Cleanup logic for it remains in the uninstall routine.  \n* **Print monitor persistence**: same pattern as COM hijack; install path absent, uninstall path retained.\n\nThe last three (registry blob loader, COM hijack, print monitor) show the opposite pattern: cleanup logic with no install logic, retained for backward compatibility against older deployments.\n\n| Feature | payloads build | Evidence |\n| ----- | ----- | ----- |\n| Direct syscalls (SSN extraction) | Active | SSN extraction \\+ stub generation confirmed |\n| AMSI / WLDP / ETW HWBP bypass | Active | `DR0`/`DR1`/`DR2` via shared helper-thread primitive |\n| Three-way process injection | Active | `PhantomInject`, `DbgNexum`, `ManualMap` are all functional |\n| Blockchain C2 resolution | Active | Three Blockscout providers queried |\n| NTDLL unhooking | **Dead code** | Strings present, zero code references |\n| HEIS encryption | **Disabled** | Code encrypt/decrypt stubbed |\n| Registry-resident PE blob loader | **Legacy only** | Only cleaned during uninstall |\n| COM hijack persistence | Legacy only | Cleaned during uninstall, never installed |\n| Print monitor persistence | Legacy only | Cleaned during uninstall, never installed |\n| Decoy strings | Active | 4 unreferenced decoy strings in `.rdata` |\n\n## Command and control\n\n### Blockchain C2 resolution\n\nPHANTOMPULSE decentralizes C2 lookup through three Blockscout providers:\n\n* `eth.blockscout[.]com` (Ethereum L1)  \n* `base.blockscout[.]com` (Base L2)  \n* `optimism.blockscout[.]com` (Optimism L2)\n\nThe wallet address `0xc117688c530b660e15085bF3A2B664117d8672aA` is XOR-decrypted from storage with a 16-byte key. For each provider, the implant issues an HTTPS GET (port 443, SSL cert errors ignored), pulls the `input` field of the latest transaction, hex-decodes it, XOR-decrypts with the wallet address bytes as the key, and validates that the result begins with `http`. On total failure, it falls back to the hardcoded URL `https://panel.fefea22134[.]net`.\n\n![C2 dexoring/decoding validation part 1](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltd7e5eae2c507f11e/6a7c79c480ee38778860d088/image16.png \"C2 dexoring/decoding validation part 1\")\n\nThe resolver does not verify the sender of the transaction. It only checks that the latest decoded `input` starts with `http`. Anyone can submit a transaction to that wallet with their own URL XOR-encoded under the wallet bytes, and every PHANTOMPULSE instance of that campaign that polls afterward resolves to that URL. For network defenders, this is a viable sinkhole at the cost of one transaction.\n\n![C2 dexoring/decoding validation part 2](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt82b8ba8304a94ae2/6a7c79c742a1170257955fbd/image24.png \"C2 dexoring/decoding validation part 2\")\n\n### Endpoints and heartbeat\n\nFive API paths are constructed at runtime, re-encrypted in memory with a per-session key:\n\n| Path | Method | Content-Type | Purpose |\n| ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |\n| `/v1/telemetry/report` | POST | `application/json` | Heartbeat with full system telemetry |\n| `/v1/telemetry/tasks/\u003cmachine_id\u003e` | GET |  | Command fetch |\n| `/v1/telemetry/upload/` | POST | `image/bmp` | Screenshot / file upload |\n| `/v1/telemetry/result` | POST | `application/json` | Command result delivery |\n| `/v1/telemetry/keylog/` | POST | `text/plain` | Keylog data upload |\n\nThe heartbeat sends a full system profile as JSON:\n\n```json\n{\n  \"machine_id\": \"\u003cuint32\u003e\",\n  \"status\": \"online\",\n  \"cpu\": \"\u003cmodel\u003e\",\n  \"gpu\": \"\u003cdescription\u003e\",\n  \"ram_mb\": \"\u003cuint32\u003e\",\n  \"os\": \"\u003cversion\u003e\",\n  \"username\": \"\u003cuser\u003e\",\n  \"computer_name\": \"\u003cname\u003e\",\n  \"cores\": \"\u003cuint32\u003e\",\n  \"screen_w\": \"\u003cint\u003e\",\n  \"screen_h\": \"\u003cint\u003e\",\n  \"privilege\": \"\u003cuser|admin|admin_nouac|system\u003e\",\n  \"build\": \"payloads\",\n  \"public_ip\": \"\u003cip\u003e\",\n  \"av_list\": [\"\u003cav1\u003e\", \"\u003cav2\u003e\"],\n  \"apps\": [\"\u003capp1\u003e\", \"\u003capp2\u003e\"],\n  \"last_cmd\": \"\u003ccmd\u003e\",\n  \"last_cmd_result\": \"\u003cresult\u003e\"\n}\n```\n\n![Building heartbeat JSON document](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltd7512d05d16f0f4b/6a7c79c9c2cc091e1124329c/image12.png \"Building heartbeat JSON document\")\n\nTwo response fields are parsed: `\"status\":\"deleted\"` triggers full uninstall; `\"ip\":\"\u003cvalue\u003e\"` populates the public-IP cache, but only if local discovery (ipif\\[.\\]org / icanhazip\\[.\\]com/ checkip.amazonaws\\[.\\]com) hasn't already filled it.\n\n![Parsing heartbeat response](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blte82878e24c1b3e6e/6a7c79cc227b1ca6c5592595/image2.png \"Parsing heartbeat response\")\n\n### Loop cadence and resilience\n\n* **Sleep**: uniform random in \\[20, 40\\] seconds  \n* **Self-healing**: runs at iteration 2, then every 10th iteration  \n* **Health-monitor tick**: first call after the implant comes online, then every 5th iteration thereafter. Populates a local system-info struct (CPU%, RAM, OS version, uptime, computer name).  \n* **Failure threshold**: 10 consecutive heartbeat failures trigger a self-restart for stuck SSL/TLS recovery  \n* **Re-resolution**: on failure, blockchain re-resolution runs; if the resolved URL changes, the failure counter resets  \n* **Public IP**: `api4.ipify[.]org` → `ipv4.icanhazip[.]com` → `checkip.amazonaws[.]com`  \n* **Connectivity check**: probes `microsoft[.]com`, `google[.]com`, `cloudflare[.]com`, `github[.]com`\n\n## Command dispatch\n\nThe command dispatcher routes commands by DJB2 hash. Eight commands total:\n\n![Command dispatch function](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt9298b63d57b5e497/6a7c79ce448e4eba7c5baa50/image25.png \"Command dispatch function\")\n\n| Hash | Command | Behavior |\n| ----- | ----- | ----- |\n| `0x04CF1142` | `inject` | Inject shellcode/DLL/EXE. Routes by type: shellcode → `PhantomInject`; DLL → `ManualMap`; EXE → `DbgNexum`. The AMSI and WLDP HWBP bypasses are installed on the first `inject` call (the ETW HWBP is already in place from `EvasionInit`). |\n| `0x7C95D91A` | `drop` | Drop the file to the disk and execute. Supports DLL, EXE, shellcode (APC injection), and MSI payloads. |\n| `0x9A37F083` | `screenshot` | GDI capture, downscale to 960px wide, upload as BMP. |\n| `0x08DEDEF0` | `keylog` | Start or stop the inline keylogger. |\n| `0x4EE251FF` | `uninstall` | 6-step cleanup and termination. |\n| `0x65CCC50B` | `elevate` | UAC bypass via the `schuac` technique (`IElevatedFactoryServer::ServerCreateElevatedObject(CLSID_TaskScheduler)`); registers a transient elevated task that relaunches the implant. |\n| `0xB3B5B880` | `downgrade` | SYSTEM → elevated admin transition. |\n| `0x20CE3BC8` | (self-restart) | Cascading self-terminate: `NtTerminateProcess(-1, 0)` direct syscall first; if that fails to resolve, falls back to `ExitProcess(0)`. Persistence relaunches the implant on the next scheduled task tick. Operationally equivalent to a soft restart. |\n\nThe eighth handler has no debug log naming it. It self-terminates; the scheduled task relaunches the implant on the next tick. The lack of LLM-style scaffolding (debug strings) makes this one of the few handlers in the binary that appears to be added by the human author rather than LLM-generated.\n\n## Injection techniques\n\nPHANTOMPULSE ships three injection techniques, one per payload type. The `inject` C2 command routes shellcode to `PhantomInject`, DLLs to `ManualMap`, and EXEs to `DbgNexum`.\n\n![Inject command switch case](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltec468e886931eeae/6a7c79d13ce8e20085cef5d6/image6.png \"Inject command switch case\")\n\nThe AMSI/WLDP hardware-breakpoint bypasses are installed on the first `inject` call, before any injector runs.\n\n| Payload type | Injector | Strategy |\n| ----- | ----- | ----- |\n| Shellcode | PhantomInject | Module stomping in `dbghelp.dll` via `SEC_IMAGE` |\n| EXE | DbgNexum | Debug-API state machine |\n| DLL | ManualMap | Full PE manual mapping |\n\n### PhantomInject: module stomping into dbghelp.dll\n\nModule stomping avoids `MEM_PRIVATE` allocation by mapping a legitimate Windows DLL as `SEC_IMAGE` and overwriting `.text`:\n\n1. Acquires `SeDebugPrivilege` (via `OpenProcessToken` / `LookupPrivilegeValueW` / `AdjustTokenPrivileges`), then walks the process snapshot for one of seven host-process candidates (case-insensitive match). Tried in priority order: `sihost.exe`, `taskhostw.exe`, `backgroundTaskHost.exe`, `RuntimeBroker.exe`, `dllhost.exe`, `ctfmon.exe`, `explorer.exe`.  \n2. Opens `dbghelp.dll` via `NtOpenFile`, creates `SEC_IMAGE` section, maps into target via `NtMapViewOfSection`\n\n   ![PhantomInject dll stomping](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt3e9d1eb62bef5a42/6a7c79d43ce8e285e6cef5da/image17.png \"PhantomInject dll stomping\")  \n3. Parses the local copy for `.text` RVA and size, then frees it  \n4. Selects and suspends a thread, captures context  \n5. Builds an [**82-byte save-call-restore trampoline**](https://gist.github.com/soolidsnake/f5cc038aa919db19658dfe4430a62114)  \n6. Writes shellcode \\+ trampoline into `.text` of the mapped DLL  \n7. Flips protection to `PAGE_EXECUTE_READ`  \n8. Repoints RIP to the trampoline, resumes thread\n\nTo a memory scanner, the result looks like a thread executing inside legitimate `dbghelp.dll`, a file-backed image region with the right file path, section name, and first-page hash.\n\n### DbgNexum: debug API as an execution controller\n\nDbgNexum handles EXE payloads. Rather than writing executable code into the target up front, it uses the Windows debug API to drive execution one exception at a time: a ROP chain whose gadgets are full Windows APIs in the target.\n\nThe technique is not original to PHANTOMPULSE. It is a verbatim lift of [`dis0rder0x00/DbgNexum`](https://github.com/dis0rder0x00/DbgNexum), a public proof of concept published on GitHub on 2026-01-04. The operator kept the published technique name (`\"DbgNexum\"`) in the implant's debug strings unchanged, and the inner state machine is a 1:1 match: the same bait API, section name, gadget chain, and constants. PHANTOMPULSE wraps the lifted x64 core with operational scaffolding the PoC does not have: host-process selection (`FindHostProcessEx`), a fallback that spawns a fresh `SysWOW64\\cmd.exe` / `rundll32.exe` / `notepad.exe`, a custom PE-loading bootstrap (so it can carry full EXEs instead of raw shellcode), and a separate WoW64 cross-architecture variant.\n\nFor native x64 payloads, the implant pre-stages the PE, the bootstrap stub, and the trampoline config inside a named file-mapping section. The section name is the literal two-byte string `\"MZ\"`, the implant attaches with `DebugActiveProcess` and creates a remote thread on `FileTimeToSystemTime` with a hardware breakpoint on DR0.\n\n![DbgNexum creates file mapping](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt09c5495fdde34784/6a7c79d71967ea750c32a773/image23.png \"DbgNexum creates file mapping\")\n\nWhen the bait hits the breakpoint, a state machine drives the target through this API chain:\n\n1. Redirect `RIP` to `DbgBreakPoint+1` with the trap flag set; the resulting single-step exception bridges into the rest of the chain.  \n2. `LocalAlloc(LMEM_ZEROINIT, 3)`, allocate the 3-byte name buffer.  \n3. `memcpy(buf, kernel32_base, 2)`, copy `\"MZ\"` from `kernel32.dll`'s DOS header into the buffer.  \n4. `memset(stack+40, 0, 8)`: zero a stack arg slot.  \n5. `OpenFileMappingA(0x1F, FALSE, \"MZ\")`, open the prepared section with full section-mapping access.  \n6. `MapViewOfFile(...)`,  map it into the target.  \n7. Redirect `RIP` to `mapped_base + 0x400`, the bootstrap stub. This is the only stage logged directly: `DbgNexumLoop64: stage 6 -\u003e stub at %llx, base=%llx`. (The PoC redirects to `mapped_base + 0` for raw shellcode; PHANTOMPULSE adds the `+0x400` offset to land on its custom PE loader.)\n\nEach transition intercepts the next debug event, restores `RSP`, clears the trap flag, modifies `RIP` and the argument registers (`RCX`, `RDX`, `R8`, `R9`) for the next call, and continues the debuggee. DR0 is reused as an execute hardware breakpoint on each saved return address, so no inline patches or `WriteProcessMemory` against the target are needed. From the kernel's view, all that happened was a thread inside `kernel32.dll` calling `LocalAlloc`, `OpenFileMappingA`, and `MapViewOfFile`.\n\nThe cross-architecture path (PE32 from a 64-bit implant) is a PHANTOMPULSE-only variant the public PoC does not have. It takes a shortcut: the implant walks the target's `PEB.Ldr` via `NtReadVirtualMemory` (using `ProcessWow64Information` to pick the 32-bit or 64-bit PEB layout) to find a DLL with a callable entry point, then pre-maps a section containing a 32-bit loader stub and trampoline into both processes via `NtCreateSection` \\+ `NtMapViewOfSection`. The redirect is just two stages: bait at `RtlExitUserThread`, then a `DbgBreakPoint`\\-mediated single step jumps `RIP` to the trampoline. There is no API chain on this path because the section is already mapped on both sides, so `OpenFileMappingA`/`MapViewOfFile` aren't needed.\n\nCross-arch host selection runs through `FindHostProcessEx`, which excludes critical system processes (`csrss.exe`, `lsass.exe`, `smss.exe`, `winlogon.exe`, `services.exe`, `wininit.exe`, `svchost.exe`, `MsMpEng.exe`) and falls back to spawning a fresh `SysWOW64\\cmd.exe` / `rundll32.exe` / `notepad.exe` if no usable WoW64 host is found.\n\n### ManualMap: full PE mapper\n\nManualMap handles DLL payloads with a complete PE manual mapping implementation:\n\n1. Validates MZ/PE signature; rejects PE32 in the x64 host path (debug-log: `\"PE32 DLL in x64 host is impossible\"`)  \n2. Allocates `SizeOfImage` in the target via `NtAllocateVirtualMemory`  \n3. Copies headers and sections to a local staging buffer  \n4. Applies base relocations (`IMAGE_REL_BASED_DIR64`, `IMAGE_REL_BASED_HIGHLOW`)  \n5. Resolves imports via `LoadLibraryA` \\+ `GetProcAddress`  \n6. Wipes PE headers (zeros `SizeOfHeaders` bytes) \n\n   ![ManualMap PE headers wiping](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt6a372965ed44ce10/6a7c79d9227b1c261859259f/image10.png \"ManualMap PE headers wiping\")\n\n7. Writes the staged image into the remote allocation  \n8. Sets per-section memory protection  \n9. Builds a **137-byte trampoline** in a separate 0x2000-byte remote allocation (set to `PAGE_EXECUTE_READ`):\n\nThe [full trampoline shellcode gist](https://gist.github.com/soolidsnake/9f8ddf9900150c467ada95ad7838bd68) contains the complete bytes.\n\n![ManualMap trampoline write](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt22431fac311cb4e5/6a7c79dc9f5251e54f663f50/image15.png \"ManualMap trampoline write\")\n\n 10\\. Hijacks a thread via suspend / get-context / set-context\n\n## Privilege escalation\n\nThe `elevate` command is a UAC bypass via the **`schuac` technique** (`IElevatedFactoryServer::ServerCreateElevatedObject(CLSID_TaskScheduler)`), published as [UACME issue \\#129](https://github.com/hfiref0x/UACME/issues/129) by zcgonvh, [currently under the ID 74](https://github.com/hfiref0x/UACME/blob/6daa8d486250c20189a803838c3654aad73a541d/README.md?plain=1#L778).\n\n![screenshot showing the ID of the privilege escalation technique under UACME](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltf7f3a234f1dd697f/6a7c79df96b5a6e8b387548c/image14.png \"screenshot showing the ID of the privilege escalation technique under UACME\")\n\n### Mechanism\n\n`MaintenanceUI.dll`'s `CMaintenanceUIVirtualFactory` (CLSID `{A6BFEA43-501F-456F-A845-983D3AD7B8F0}`) is registered with an `Elevation` registry key, so the OS hands non-admin callers an elevated instance. Its `IElevatedFactoryServer` interface exposes `ServerCreateElevatedObject(rclsid, riid, ppv)`, which the elevated server uses to instantiate **any other CLSID** under its elevated context. PHANTOMPULSE feeds it `CLSID_TaskScheduler`, gets back an elevated `ITaskService`, and uses that service to register a `HighestAvailable`\\-RunLevel task that re-launches the implant.\n\n### The `elevate` C2 command\n\nInside `ProcessCommands`, the elevate handler:\n\n1. Builds the task action. The command is `\u003csystem_dir\u003e\\rundll32.exe` with arguments `\\\"\u003cdeployed_dll\u003e\\\",DllRegisterServer`. The user identifier is `COMPUTERNAME\\USERNAME` from `GetEnvironmentVariableW`. ![Elevate command call][/assets/images/blockchain-c2-phantompulse-rat-sinkhole/image17.png]\n2. Writes the `.elevate` marker as a single byte (`\"1\"`, `0x31`), not encoded parameters. The write goes through `NtCreateFile` + `NtWriteFile` to bypass user-mode hooks. The marker is just a presence flag; the elevation parameters travel inside the task definition the implant is about to register.\n3. XOR-decrypts the COM Elevation Moniker at runtime, 66 bytes from `.rdata` xored against seed `0xE95CA237`, which decodes to `Elevation:Administrator!new:{A6BFEA43-501F-456F-A845-983D3AD7B8F0}`.\n4. Calls `CoGetObject(moniker, \u0026BIND_OPTS3{dwClassContext=CLSCTX_LOCAL_SERVER}, IID_IElevatedFactoryServer, \u0026factory)` to get an elevated `IElevatedFactoryServer*`, then `factory-\u003eServerCreateElevatedObject(CLSID_TaskScheduler, IID_ITaskService, \u0026elevatedTaskService)` to get an elevated `ITaskService*` that inherits the elevation.\n5. Registers a transient task `DotNetSvcElevateTask` at `HighestAvailable` RunLevel with the rundll32 action above.\n6. Deletes any pre-existing non-elevated persistent tasks so the old low-IL persistence cannot race the elevated relaunch.\n7. Calls `ITaskService::Run` on the transient task and deletes it immediately afterward. Releases the single-instance mutex and exits the medium-IL implant.\n\n\n### Fallback retry via proxy rundll32\n\nIf the `CoGetObject` / `RegisterTask` chain fails, a fallback path takes over. A separate startup path spawns a fresh `rundll32.exe \"\u003cdeployed_dll\u003e\",DllRegisterServer` directly via `CreateProcessW`, with retries (`\"\u003e\u003e\u003e .elevate redirect attempt %d\"`). Inside that rundll32 the implant detects `isProxy=1`, `elevated=0` and **retries the schuac sequence** with three registration variants (`ELEVATED+INTERACTIVE+user`, `ELEVATED+INTERACTIVE`, `INTERACTIVE`). On success, the elevated task fires and the elevated relaunch takes over. On exhaustion, the proxy logs `\"\u003e\u003e\u003e Phase 1: all registration methods failed, cleaning marker\"` and exits.\n\n### Elevated rundll32 relaunch\n\nWhen the transient task fires, `svchost.exe (Schedule)` launches `rundll32.exe \"\u003cdeployed_dll\u003e\",DllRegisterServer` under a fresh high-IL token. The implant's `DllRegisterServer` export runs as the entry; at startup it sees the `.elevate` marker and a high-IL token and routes to the elevated path:\n\n* Reads and **deletes** the `.elevate` marker.  \n* **Reinstalls persistence under elevated context**, including the boot task `DotNetSvcCoreTask` under `\\Microsoft\\Windows\\NetFramework\\`, registered with `INTERACTIVE_TOKEN` \\+ `BootTrigger`, which requires admin to register.  \n* Continues normal implant operation as a high-IL service.\n\n### Marker-state routing\n\nAt every startup, `MainEntryLogic` calls `GetFileAttributesW` on the `.elevate` path. If it returns `INVALID_FILE_ATTRIBUTES`, the implant skips all elevation logic and starts normally. If the marker exists, the implant gathers two more facts: whether the current process is a `rundll32.exe`/`regsvr32.exe` proxy, and whether the token is elevated, then routes on the combination:\n\n| Marker | Proxy | Elevated | What happens |\n| ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |\n| absent | n/a | n/a | normal startup, no special routing |\n| present | no | no | spawn proxy rundll32 to retry the schuac chain, then exit |\n| present | yes | no | retry schuac inside the rundll32; on exhaustion delete marker and exit |\n| present | \\* | yes | delete marker, reinstall persistence with the boot task, continue as high-IL |\n\nThe marker file's *contents* are never read, its presence alone drives the routing.\n\nElastic Security Labs' [Exploring Windows UAC Bypasses](https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/exploring-windows-uac-bypasses-techniques-and-detection-strategies) covers detection patterns for the `IElevatedFactoryServer` class of bypass directly.\n\n## Persistence\n\n### Three scheduled tasks\n\nPHANTOMPULSE installs three scheduled tasks via the COM `ITaskService` interface, each executing `rundll32.exe \"\u003cstub_dll\u003e\",DllRegisterServer`:\n\n| Task | Trigger | Interval | RunLevel |\n| ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |\n| `DotNetSvcUpdateTask` | User Logon \\+ Time | 3 min | Standard |\n| `DotNetSvcCoreTask` | Boot \\+ Time | 15 min | HighestAvailable \\+ Hidden |\n| `DotNetSvcUserTask` | User Logon | On logon | Standard |\n\n![Persistance install start](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltcd3069226811430b/6a7c79e2e02fac76565d03e3/image5.png \"Persistance install start\")\n\nThe boot task lives under `\\Microsoft\\Windows\\NetFramework\\` to blend with legitimate .NET maintenance tasks. Its full XML body is embedded in `.rdata`, not constructed at runtime. Signatures on this literal blob are durable across builds.\n\nInstallation tries multiple `RegisterTask` fallback variants per task, combining `INTERACTIVE_TOKEN` and `S4U` logon types with different user-identifier formats (`COMPUTER\\User`, `SID`, short username). The exact order of attempts depends on the implant's privilege context (SYSTEM, admin, or standard user); failures fall through to the next variant until one succeeds.\n\n### Stub DLL drop\n\nThe deployed agent lives **encrypted and compressed inside the EXE's own `.rdata`**. `FilelessPersist` decrypts it on demand and writes the result to disk via (`NtCreateFile` \\+ `NtWriteFile`).\n\nDecryption is two stages:\n\n1. **XOR-decode** a blob against a rotating 16-byte key at (`decoded[i] = blob[i] ^ key[i \u0026 0xF]`, with `decoded[0] = 0x1E` as a hard-coded first byte that primes the LZNT1 chunk header).  \n2. **`RtlDecompressBuffer(COMPRESSION_FORMAT_LZNT1, ...)`** on the decoded buffer, which is a DLL (`svcagent.dll`).\n\nDrop paths in priority order, falling through on access errors:\n\n1. `%ProgramData%\\AssetMon\\svcagent.dll` (primary)  \n2. `%APPDATA%\\AssetMon\\svcagent.dll`  \n3. `%TEMP%\\svcagent.dll`  \n4. A redundant `%ProgramData%` \"sleeper\" copy at a separate path\n\nAnalysts can reproduce the deployed DLL offline by reading the two regions above out of the EXE, applying the XOR loop, and feeding the result to `RtlDecompressBuffer` (or any LZNT1 implementation) as seen in the CyberChef screenshot below.\n\n![Cyberchef decrypting the DLL](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt724d8f9adc74140f/6a7c79e533fa8abba21fc841/image19.png \"Cyberchef decrypting the DLL\")\n\n### DLL sideload migration\n\nA block inside `SetupRegistryPE` (logged with the `MigrateSideload` / `MigrateLegacySideloads` debug-string prefixes) enumerates running processes and their **executable directories**, hunting for `diagcore.dll`. When found, it overwrites the file with the current stub via `CopyFileW`.\n\n### Self-healing\n\nSelf-healing runs on iteration 2 of the C2 loop and every 10th iteration thereafter, gated on a deferred-persist flag being clear. The check order:  \n![Self healing start](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt0b2a7386cb663a47/6a7c79e82f00b24effef8d55/image18.png \"Self healing start\")\n\n1. **Registry-persistence check first.** `CheckRegistryPersistence` runs at the top of the block. If it reports unhealthy, the implant immediately re-runs `FilelessPersist` (re-decrypts and re-drops the stub DLL) and `InstallPersistence` (re-registers the task triggers).  \n2. **Task verification.** `SelfHealCheckTasks` then verifies the three persistence tasks (`DotNetSvcUpdateTask`, `DotNetSvcCoreTask`, `DotNetSvcUserTask`) and reinstalls any that are missing. The boot task check is gated on SYSTEM-or-admin context; non-privileged callers skip it.  \n3. **AV inventory refresh.** `DetectInstalledAV` runs at the end to refresh the operator-visible AV product list.\n\nThe privilege gating matters for eviction. From a non-elevated context, the boot-task check is skipped, so the boot task is not inspected during cleanup. Full eviction requires removing all three tasks plus the registry artifact in one window from an elevated context.\n\nBeyond the iteration-based self-heal, the implant carries a **deferred-persist mechanism** keyed off a single flag. On heartbeat-success paths in `C2Loop_Main`, once the heartbeat-success counter exceeds one with the flag set, the implant re-runs `FilelessPersist` \\+ `InstallPersistence` and clears the flag. This gives PHANTOMPULSE a second persistence-repair path that fires on a different trigger than the iteration-based self-heal.\n\n## Collection\n\n### Inline keylogger with clipboard monitoring\n\nThe keylogger runs inline in the C2 loop with no dedicated thread. It resolves APIs from `user32.dll` at runtime:\n\n| API | Purpose |\n| ----- | ----- |\n| `GetAsyncKeyState` | Polling key state |\n| `GetForegroundWindow` | Active window detection |\n| `GetWindowTextA` | Window title capture |\n| `MapVirtualKeyA` / `ToUnicode` | Key translation |\n| `GetClipboardSequenceNumber` | Clipboard change detection |\n| `OpenClipboard` / `GetClipboardData` | Clipboard reading (CF\\_UNICODETEXT) |\n\n![Keylogger function](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt492b0d89f5883d9d/6a7c79ebbd219804857521a6/image21.png \"Keylogger function\")\n\nThe log file is XOR-encrypted with the `0xE95CA237` seed. Uploads send only the delta to avoid retransmission.\n\n### Screenshot\n\nScreenshots use GDI APIs resolved by hash. If desktop width exceeds 960 px, the image is downscaled before upload. The raw BMP is built in memory and uploaded with `Content-Type: image/bmp`. Triggered on-demand by the `screenshot` C2 command (hash `0x9A37F083`).\n\n![Screenshot function](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt18af9d7b39c4d560/6a7c79ef227b1c35835925af/image27.png \"Screenshot function\")\n\n### System reconnaissance\n\nRecon data the implant gathers:\n\n| Data | Source |\n| ----- | ----- |\n| CPU | Registry: `ProcessorNameString` |\n| GPU | Registry display adapter `DriverDesc` (filters \"Microsoft Basic\") |\n| RAM | `GlobalMemoryStatusEx` |\n| OS | `RtlGetVersion` with build-to-version mapping (Win7 through Win11, Server 2008 through Server 2025\\) |\n| Username | `GetUserNameW` with fallback to `LookupAccountSidW` from `explorer.exe` token |\n| Privilege | Token elevation type: `user`, `admin`, `admin_nouac`, `system` |\n| AV | `DetectInstalledAV` matches running processes against a hardcoded list of \\~25–30 AV vendor process names |\n| Apps | `DetectInstalledApps` checks a curated 19-name targeted-app list |\n| Firewall state | Reads `SYSTEM\\CurrentControlSet\\Services\\SharedAccess\\Parameters\\FirewallPolicy\\{Domain,Standard,Public}Profile` to record per-profile enabled state |\n| Services | Running-service count via service enumeration |\n| Machine ID | DJB2(module name) ^ volume serial |\n| Public IP | Multi-API HTTPS chain |\n\n![System reconnaissance](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt1bb3a0359b1da48b/6a7c79f23ce8e28793cef5f2/image1.png \"System reconnaissance\")\n\nThe AV-detection list is unusually broad, covering standard Western consumer AV products such as Defender, Norton, McAfee, Avast, AVG, Avira, Bitdefender, ESET, F-Secure, G Data, Kaspersky, Panda, Sophos, Trend Micro, VIPRE, Webroot, ZoneAlarm, Comodo along with EDR vendors (CrowdStrike, SentinelOne, Cylance, Malwarebytes, HitmanPro) are all covered. The implant also probes for **AhnLab V3** (South Korean), **Qihoo 360 / 360 Total Security** and **Tencent QQPC** (Chinese), and **K7 Computing** (Indian). The Asian-AV inclusion is uncommon for Western-targeted commodity stealers and consistent with an implant designed for victims across multiple regional markets.\n\nThe implant also checks for a curated list of 19 high-value applications by name and flags matches in the heartbeat (`App detection: found %d apps`):\n\n| Category | Targets |\n| ----- | ----- |\n| Cryptocurrency wallets | `ledger`, `trezor`, `bitcoin-core`, `electrum`, `exodus`, `atomic`, `guarda` |\n| Messengers | `telegram`, `discord`, `signal`, `viber`, `slack`, `whatsapp` |\n| Mail clients | `thunderbird`, `outlook` |\n| 2FA app | `authy` |\n| File transfer / SSH | `filezilla`, `winscp` |\n| Gaming | `steam` |\n\nThe detection function (`DetectInstalledApps`) does **not** scan the registry or enumerate processes. It expands three environment-variable roots (`%LOCALAPPDATA%`, `%APPDATA%`, `%ProgramFiles(x86)%`), concatenates a hardcoded UTF-16 relative-path suffix per app (e.g. `\\Telegram Desktop\\`, `\\Authy Desktop\\`, `\\Ledger Live\\`, `\\@trezor\\trezor-suite\\`, `\\Steam\\steam.exe`), and calls `GetFileAttributesW` on each path. A non-error return means the app is installed, and the name is recorded in the heartbeat results buffer.\n\n![List of Targeted apps](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltbd4606baacbce172/6a7c79f5bd21980acb7521b0/image8.png \"List of Targeted apps\")\n\nPHANTOMPULSE itself does not extract data from any of these. The list is target reconnaissance for follow-on tasking. The operator sees in the heartbeat which high-value applications a given victim has and decides what specialized payload to push next via `inject` or `drop`.\n\nNo wallet, browser, messenger, or credential stealer functionality was identified in the analyzed sample; the targeting list is purely a presence-check feeding the operator's decision tree.\n\n## Uninstall\n\nA 6-step cleanup, triggered by the `uninstall` command, by `\"status\":\"deleted\"` in a heartbeat response, or by a kill flag in the registry:\n\n| Step | Action |\n| ----- | ----- |\n| 1/6 | Write kill flag to HKCU \\+ HKLM, kill host process |\n| 2/6 | Remove all 3 scheduled tasks via COM \\+ `CreateProcessW` fallback |\n| 3/6 | Remove legacy registry: NTLoad value, COM hijack keys, print monitor keys |\n| 4/6 | Delete stub DLLs, sleeper logs, registry PE blob, ProgramData directories |\n| 5/6 | Delete install path and self path from disk |\n| 6/6 | Terminate residual `healthmon.exe` and any `rundll32.exe` instances hosting `svcagent.dll` |\n\nStep 3 reveals the legacy persistence techniques: cleanup logic for COM hijack and print monitor keys that this build never installs.\n\n![Removing persistance through uninstall command](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt2906693b11867a63/6a7c79f88fc2d008203e87d2/image3.png \"Removing persistance through uninstall command\")\n\n## Attribution\n\nPHANTOMPULSE's tradecraft, targeting, and infrastructure choices align with the **DPRK-aligned crypto-targeting intrusion clusters** that include Lazarus, BlueNoroff, UNC5342 (Contagious Interview), and APT38. Multiple independent dimensions match recent public reporting on those clusters.\n\n**Signals aligning with DPRK reporting:**\n\n* **Blockchain-resolved C2 via transaction `input` fields** matches the dead-drop-resolver pattern Mandiant attributes to UNC5342 (Contagious Interview) in [DPRK Adopts EtherHiding](https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/dprk-adopts-etherhiding). PHANTOMPULSE's specifics (wallet-byte XOR, multi-chain Blockscout) are not a 1:1 fingerprint, but the technique class is now DPRK-tagged.  \n* **Desktop crypto-wallet enumeration set** (`ledger`, `trezor`, `bitcoin-core`, `electrum`, `exodus`, `atomic`, `guarda`) closely matches Unit 42's [RustDoor / Koi Stealer for macOS](https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/macos-malware-targets-crypto-sector/) targeting list, which is DPRK-attributed.  \n* **Cross-platform Windows \\+ macOS implants** for the same victim profile (the prior REF6598 post documented a macOS sibling with C2 at `0x666[.]info` and a Telegram fallback at `t[.]me/ax03bot`) is a BlueNoroff signature.  \n* **Telegram and messenger targeting** is specifically a BlueNoroff specialty per [Arctic Wolf BlueNoroff coverage](https://arcticwolf.com/resources/blog/bluenoroff-uses-clickfix-fileless-powershell-and-ai-generated-zoom-meetings-to-target-web3-sector/).\n\n## Hunting for new C2 domains via the resolver wallet's known-plaintext signature\n\nThe XOR scheme used by the blockchain resolver leaks a stable 2-byte signature defenders can hunt against the entire chain, not just one wallet.\n\nTwo facts combine: every C2 URL begins with `ht` (from `http://` or `https://`), and the XOR key is the wallet's ASCII address verbatim, so its first two key bytes are always the literal characters `0` and `x`. XOR-ing `ht` against `0x` yields `\\x58 \\x0c`. **Every encrypted `input` field produced by a PHANTOMPULSE-style resolver, on any chain, signed by any related wallet, begins with the four hex characters `580c`.**\n\nThis converts the hunt from monitoring one wallet into sweeping the chain for the signature. Public Ethereum, Base, and Optimism transaction data is queryable via BigQuery, Dune, or full archive nodes. A query against the public Ethereum transactions dataset for `input` values starting with `0x580c`, scoped to a recent block-timestamp window, surfaces previously-unknown resolver wallets used by the same codebase. Each match is validated by decoding with the sender wallet's ASCII address as the key: a real C2 URL begins with `http` after decoding. The following [CyberChef recipe](https://gchq.github.io/CyberChef/#recipe=From_Hex\\('Auto'\\)XOR\\(%7B'option':'UTF8','string':''%7D,'Standard',false\\)) can be used to decrypt the C2 URL.\n\n```sql\nSELECT \n    block_timestamp AS block_time, \n    from_address AS `from`, \n    to_address AS `to`, \n    input AS data\nFROM `bigquery-public-data.crypto_ethereum.transactions`\nWHERE block_timestamp \u003e= '2026-04-01 00:00:00'\n  AND input LIKE '0x580c%'\nORDER BY block_timestamp DESC\nLIMIT 10000;\n```\n\n![Bigquery on Etherium transactions](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt0310872d35c65f2c/6a7c79fb8fc2d09d673e87da/image20.png \"Bigquery on Etherium transactions\")\n\nCyberChef can decrypt the input data to reveal the domain, as shown in the screenshot below.\n\n![Cyberchef to decrypt the C2](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt5b62dbd8c55c9d16/6a7c79feda3d0589cf633c78/image4.png \"Cyberchef to decrypt the C2\")\n\n## Conclusion\n\nPHANTOMPULSE is engineered from published components: module stomping, debug-API state machines, manual mapping, hardware-breakpoint AMSI/WLDP/ETW bypass, scheduled-task persistence, and blockchain C2. The combination and the hardening that ties it together point to a mature codebase under active development. The durable signals are behavioral and are covered by Elastic's [behavioral protections for REF6598](https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/phantom-in-the-vault).\n\n## PHANTOMPULSE and MITRE ATT\\\u0026CK\n\nElastic uses the MITRE ATT\\\u0026CK framework to document common tactics, techniques, and procedures that advanced persistent threats use against enterprise networks.\n\n### Tactics\n\nTactics represent the why of a technique or sub-technique. It is the adversary's tactical goal: the reason for performing an action.\n\n* [Initial Access](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0001/)  \n* [Execution](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0002/)  \n* [Persistence](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0003/)  \n* [Privilege Escalation](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0004/)  \n* [Defense Evasion](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0005/)  \n* [Credential Access](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0006/)  \n* [Discovery](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0007/)  \n* [Collection](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0009/)  \n* [Command and Control](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0011/)  \n* [Exfiltration](https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0010/)\n\n### Techniques\n\nTechniques represent how an adversary achieves a tactical goal by performing an action.\n\n* [Phishing: Spearphishing via Service](https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1566/003/)  \n* [Command and Scripting Interpreter: Analysis","url":"/security-labs/threat-command/category/malware-analysis","locale":"en-us","created_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","updated_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","created_at":"2026-08-12T12:28:35.178Z","updated_at":"2026-08-14T14:34:54.590Z","ACL":{},"_version":2,"tags":[],"seo":{"canonical_tag":"","do_not_index":false,"nofollow":false,"seo_description_l10n":"","seo_image":null,"seo_title_l10n":""},"title_l10n":"Malware Analysis","publish_details":{"time":"2026-08-25T08:19:17.315Z","user":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","environment":"blt835e2c3b7e9eb7fb","locale":"en-us"}}],"created_at":"2026-08-12T15:48:20.752Z","created_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","date":"2026-05-07T00:00:00.000Z","main_content":{"markdown_l10n":"Elastic Security Labs identified a new Brazilian banking trojan that we are tracking as TCLBANKER, a malware family we assess is a major update of the [MAVERICK](https://securelist.com/maverick-banker-distributing-via-whatsapp/117715/)/[SORVEPOTEL](https://www.trendmicro.com/en_us/research/25/j/self-propagating-malware-spreads-via-whatsapp.html) family. The campaign, tracked as REF3076, features a loader with robust anti-analysis capabilities that deploys two embedded .NET Reactor-protected modules: a full-featured banking trojan and a worm module for self-propagation.\n\nThe banking trojan monitors the victim's browser address bar via UI Automation, targeting 59 Brazilian banking, fintech, and cryptocurrency domains. Beyond the usual remote access commands, its most notable capability is a WPF-based full-screen overlay framework designed for operator-driven social engineering. \n\nA second module handles distribution through spam agents, of which we recovered two variants: a WhatsApp worm that hijacks authenticated browser sessions to message the victim's contacts, and an Outlook email bot that sends phishing emails through the victim's own accounts via COM automation.\n\nThrough this report, we provide a detailed technical breakdown of each stage.\n\n## Key takeaways\n\n* TCLBANKER uses environment-gated payload decryption; incorrect environments, such as sandboxes, silently fail to decrypt the payload  \n* A comprehensive watchdog subsystem continuously monitors for analysis tools, debuggers, instrumentation frameworks, and integrity violations throughout execution  \n* The banking trojan targets 59 Brazilian banking, fintech, and cryptocurrency domains, activating a WebSocket C2 session when a victim navigates to a monitored site  \n* A WPF-based full-screen overlay framework enables operator-driven social engineering, including credential harvesting, vishing wait screens, and fake Windows Update stalls, while hiding overlays from screen capture tools  \n* Worm modules propagate the malware: a WhatsApp bot and an Outlook email bot  \n* All C2 and distribution infrastructure is hosted on Cloudflare Workers under a single account, with developer artifacts (debug logging paths, test process names) and an incomplete phishing page, suggesting the campaign was identified in an early operational stage\n\n## Delivery\n\nTCLBANKER is a Brazilian banking trojan that contains a dynamic infection chain with a heavy anti-analysis loading component that can deploy two embedded payloads (worm, banker). The observed infection chain bundles a malicious MSI installer inside a ZIP file. These MSI installer packages are abusing a signed Logitech program called [Logi AI Prompt Builder](https://www.logitech.com/en-us/software/logi-ai-prompt-builder).\n\n![MSI install dialog](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltb735d131b89aa381/6a7c955a448e4e736b5bafc4/image13.png \"MSI install dialog\")  \n\nTCLBANKER abuses DLL sideloading against `LogiAiPromptBuilder.exe`, a legitimate Logitech application built on the [Flutter](https://flutter.dev/) framework. The malicious DLL `screen_retriever_plugin.dll` masquerades as a legitimate Flutter plugin of the same name and is loaded automatically when the host application starts.\n\n![File directory contents showing a malicious DLL](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltdafccb8ce5eaf7f2/6a7c955dbd219806fe7526ab/image32.png \"File directory contents showing a malicious DLL\")  \n\nAfter the MSI installation, the malicious DLL is immediately loaded and starts at the DllMain entry point. \n\n## Loader\n\nThe loader component for TCLBANKER is packed with features, including anti-debugging features, anti-analysis checks, string encryption, system language checks, ETW patching, and a watchdog capability. While it has many features, it lacks depth and has references to older malware analysis tooling. It’s not entirely clear whether the developer used LLM-assisted workflows, but our team wouldn’t be surprised if that were the case.\n\nAt the beginning of the execution, TCLBANKER aligns the corresponding .NET assembly payloads based on whether the string (`--renderer=sw`) is used in the command-line. Within its main loader function, it first performs allow-list/blocklist operations based on how the DLL was loaded. The malicious DLL will only execute if the host process comes from the following two processes:\n\n- `logiaipromptbuilder.exe`   \n- `tclloader.exe` (Possible reference to developer string during testing)\n\nIf the DLL was loaded by the following processes, it will refuse to run. These processes are traditionally used by analysts to load and debug DLLs.\n\n- `rundll32.exe`  \n- `regsvr32.exe`  \n- `dllhost.exe`  \n- `svchost.exe`\n\nNext, TCLBBANKER removes any user-mode hooking by replacing `ntdll.dll` from disk. For more evasion, the malware generates the following syscall trampolines used later:\n\n- `NtQueryInformationProcess`  \n- `NtSetInformationThread`  \n- `NtSetInformationProcess`  \n- `NtTerminateProcess`  \n- `NtAllocateVirtualMemory`  \n- `NtProtectVirtualMemory`\n\nAfter installing syscall stubs, the malware patches `EtwEventWrite` in `ntdll.dll` with the classic `xor eax, eax; ret` to disable user-mode ETW telemetry.\n\n![Patching via EtwEventWrite](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt6ac9546af6343006/6a7c9560e3a2195c9e999b56/image33.png \"Patching via EtwEventWrite\")  \n\nTCLBANKER performs an initial sandbox check by capturing a start tick using `GetTickCount64()`, sleeping for 500 ms, and measuring the elapsed time. If fewer than 450 ms have actually passed, the malware bails — this detects sandboxes or emulation frameworks that hook Sleep to return immediately..\n\n![Sandbox check](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt0f9ce832e3f76fab/6a7c956305b7b551df185e7d/image9.png \"Sandbox check\")\n\nOne of the more interesting features of TCLBANKER is an enumeration function that generates three  fingerprints based on the following criteria:\n\n- Anti-debugging checks  \n- System disk information and memory checks  \n- Language checks\n\nThe developer uses magic constants assigned to “clean” paths for each category, then performs an XOR against each one to generate the environment hash. This environment hash value is significant because it affects downstream decryption of the embedded payload. \n\n![Environmental hashing function](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt7969ec49d2084865/6a7c9565c2cc09dcd42437c9/image4.png \"Environmental hashing function\")  \n\nFor example, if a debugger is present, it will produce an incorrect hash, so when the malware attempts to derive the decryption keys from the hash, the payload will not decrypt correctly, and TCLBANKER will stop executing.\n\n![Decryption derivation function using gated hash value](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt4bdace3d45937dd8/6a7c956873d9bd4ed429801e/image19.png \"Decryption derivation function using gated hash value\")\n\n## Anti-debugging checks\n\nTCLBANKER implements six different anti-debugging checks:\n\n- Identify the debugger through the `Peb-\u003eBeingDebugged` flag  \n- Checks heap-tail/heap-free/check-heap flags set when a process is launched under a debugger  \n- Leverages `NtQueryInformationProcess()` using `ProcessDebugPort`  \n- Uses `NtQueryInformationProcess()` using `ProcessDebugObjectHandle`  \n- Hardware breakpoint detection via the debug registers (`DR0-DR3`)  \n- Measures the elapsed time using `QueryPerformanceCounter()` deltas and `RDTSC` cycle counts\n\n![Anti-debugging checks with calculation](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt8730544fc5209ec1/6a7c956a33fa8a2ed41fcd99/image30.png \"Anti-debugging checks with calculation\")  \n\n## System information checks\n\nTCLBANKER has the following five different checks based on virtualization, system, and user information:\n\n- Checks for virtualization software using vendor signature\n\n| Hypervisor | Vendor signature |\n| :---: | :---: |\n| VMware | VMwareVMware |\n| VirtualBox | VBoxVBoxVBox |\n| KVM | KVMKVMKVM |\n| Xen | XenVMMXenVMM |\n| Parallels | prl hyperv |\n| QEMU/TCG | TCGTCGTCGTCG |\n\n- Verify the root system drive (`C:\\\\`) via `GetDiskFreeSpaceExW()` has at least 64 GB  \n- Calls `GlobalMemoryStatusEx()` to verify the system has more than 2 GB of RAM  \n- Checks for 2 or CPU processors via `GetSystemInfo()`  \n- Checks for generic sandbox/malware usernames  \n  - `sandbox`, `malware`, `virus`, `sample`, `john doe`, `currentuser`\n\n![System enumeration checks](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt184203be83734ca9/6a7c956d448e4e2b265bafc8/image28.png \"System enumeration checks\")  \n\n## Language checks\n\nFor the last environment fingerprint check, TCLBANKER retrieves geographical information of the infected machine using `GetUserGeoID()`, targeting Brazilian users based on the geographical ID (`0x20`). A second locale check via `GetUserDefaultLCID()` also ensures the user's default language is Brazilian Portuguese (pt-BR, LANGID 0x0416).\n\n![Language check targeting Brazilian users](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt4b4d38cf0bf38093/6a7c9570227b1c7e28592b17/image11.png \"Language check targeting Brazilian users\")  \n\nAfter these sets of checks, TCLBANKER will either bail out of execution if anything is detected or, if not, produce another anti-debugging check by patching `DbgUiRemoteBreakin()`. The malware patches its first byte to `a ret` instruction so that any attempt to remotely break into the process does nothing — the injected thread immediately returns, and the target keeps running, unsuspended.\n\n![Patching `DbgUiRemoteBreakin`](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt7c3deac601aec711/6a7c957377b034b8263f99a9/image18.png \"Patching `DbgUiRemoteBreakin`\")  \n\nAfter this, the malware derives an AES-256 CBC key and IV by using hard-coded constants from the `.rdata` section along with the environmental hash calculated earlier. TCLBanker uses `BCryptDecrypt()` to decrypt the embedded payload, then decompresses via `RtlDecompressBuffer` using the LZNT1 compression algorithm.\n\nOnce the respective payload is decrypted, TCLBANKER initializes COM via `CoInitializeEx()` and uses the CLR hosting APIs to load the .NET runtime in-process. Before launching the payload entry point, TCLBanker creates two new threads: one serves as the watchdog, and the other monitors the watchdog thread as a heartbeat check. \n\n## Watchdog\n\nTCLBANKER has a comprehensive watchdog feature that targets various analysis tools, including disassemblers, debuggers, instrumentation products, anti-virus products, and sandbox products. This section will outline the various techniques used by this feature:\n\n- Debugger check via `PEB→BeingDebugged`  \n- Watches for hardware breakpoints `DR0`/`DR1`/`DR2`/`DR3`  \n- Checks Windows functions (`BCryptDecrypt()`, `BCryptOpenAlgorithmProvider()`) for in-line hooks by scanning the first 12 bytes of each function  \n- Monitors for instrumentation tools and related strings (`frida`, `cydia`, `user-path injection`, `hook framework`)  \n- Reviews all kernel named pipes searching for `frida` or `linjector`  \n- Performs process enumeration via `CreateToolhelp32Snapshot()` targeting the following process names:  \n  - `frida`, `de4dot`, `dnspy`, `megadumper`, `extremedumper`, `processhacker`, `x64dbg`, `x32dbg`, `pe-sieve`, `scylla`, `Ilspy`, `dotpeek`, `netreactorslayer`, `cheatengine`\n\n![Targeted process names decrypted by TCLBANKER](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt7658a02f9564a64d/6a7c95763ce8e27c18cefb08/image1.png \"Targeted process names decrypted by TCLBANKER\")  \n\n- Employs Windows title detection via `GetWindowTextW()` with these titles:  \n  - `x64dbg`, `x32dbg`, `ida -`, `ida pro`, `ghidra`, `dnspy`, `megadumper`, `extremedumper`, `processhacker`, `ollydbg`, `windbg)_`, `pe_sieve`, `scylla`  \n- Identifies analyst tooling based on the following window class names via `FindWindowW()`:  \n  - `IDATopLevelWindow`, `idaabortwndclass`, `TIdaWindow`, `x64dbg`, `x32dbg`, `OLLYDBG`, `WinDbgFrameClass`, `ProcessHacker`, `SystemInformer`, `CheatEngine`, `HxdClass`  \n- Checks for the following loaded modules  \n  - `dbeng.dll`, `dbgcore.dll`, `SbieDll.dll`, `snxhk.dll`, `cmdvrt32.dll`, `cmdvrt64.dll`, `cuckoomon.dll`, `pstorec.dll`, `vmcheck.dll`, `wpespy.dll`  \n- Targets the following mutexes and events:  \n  - `Ida_trusted_idbs`, `IDA_COMM_PIPE_`, `Local\\\\x64dbg`, `Local\\\\x32dbg`, `Frida`, `YOURAPPNAMEHERE`  \n- Performs `CRC32` integrity check on the `.text` section to prevent any tampering\n\n## Banking Trojan Module\n\n`Tcl.Agent` is a banking trojan, the main component of the chain. It is .NET Reactor-protected, and although we failed to deobfuscate it using available open-source tooling such as de4dot and NETReactorSlayer, we managed to statically deobfuscate this stage up to a satisfiable state using a custom deobfuscation pipeline to tackle .NET Reactor’s string encryption, control flow flattening, IL mutation, delegate proxies, and encrypted method bodies (Necrobit). Although it is a new malware, much of the code structure still follows ESET’s LATAM banking trojan [implementation blueprint](https://web-assets.esetstatic.com/wls/2020/09/ESET_LATAM_financial_cybercrime.pdf), published in 2020.\n\nAt start, the malware performs geofencing, requiring \u003e= 2 of the following indicators to match Brazil; otherwise, it exits immediately if not on a Brazilian machine:\n\n| Check | Implementation |\n| :---- | :---- |\n| Region Code | new RegionInfo(CultureInfo.CurrentCulture.LCID).TwoLetterISORegionName == \"BR\" |\n| Timezone | TimeZoneInfo.Local.BaseUtcOffset.TotalHours: if \u003e= -5.0, check if == -2.0 |\n| LCID | CultureInfo.CurrentCulture.LCID == 1046 (Portuguese-Brazil) |\n| Keyboard | GetKeyboardLayoutList() - check each layout: (ToInt32() \u0026 0xFFFF) == 1046 |\n\n## Installation and Persistence\n\nOn the first run, the malware copies the entire application directory into `%LocalAppData%\\LogiAI`. It computes a SHA-256 hash over all `.dll` and `.exe` files in the source directory and writes it to a `.version` marker file. On subsequent runs, it compares hashes to skip redundant copies. After copying, it launches the new instance from the install path and exits.\n\nIt creates a scheduled task named `RuntimeOptimizeService` using COM interop with the Task Scheduler (`CLSID 0F87369F-A4E5-4CFC-BD3E-73E6154572DD`). The task is configured as hidden, enabled, with no execution time limit, allowed on battery, start-when-available, and fires on a logon trigger (`type 9`) scoped to the current user. It registers with `TASK_CREATE_OR_UPDATE` and `TASK_LOGON_SERVICE_ACCOUNT`.\n\n![Register task for persistence](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt8ac0bec7b56dbc6d/6a7c957905b7b51289185e83/image12.png \"Register task for persistence\")  \n\nAfter persistence is established, the agent sends a first-run POST beacon to `https://campanha1-api.ef971a42.workers[.]dev/api/installs` with the agentId (`MachineName-UserName`), MachineName, UserName (redundant), and the OS version. The request is authenticated with a hardcoded campaign authentication token `0d21613a-2609-45fc-83ff-d0feaa0c891f`. The newer variant adds debug logging around this call (`C:\\temp\\tcl-debug.txt`), a developer artifact that inadvertently exposes agent presence on disk.\n\n![Initial beacon to indicate successful installation](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt56da1bf5dcae05f2/6a7c957b3ce8e264d4cefb0c/image22.png \"Initial beacon to indicate successful installation\")  \n\n## Self-Update\n\nThe agent implements a hash-based self-update gate that runs early in the startup pipeline. It reads a local version hash from `flutter_engine.cfg` in its install directory (migrating from a legacy `version.hash` filename if present), then fetches the current hash from the file server endpoint `documents.ef971a42.workers[.]dev/api/version` using a truncated User-Agent string `(Mozilla/5.0 (Windows NT 10.0; Win64; x64) AppleWebKit/537.36`. \n\nThe response is parsed for the \"hash\" key. If the remote hash matches the local hash, execution continues normally. On first install, the remote hash is written to disk, and execution proceeds without updating.\n\n![Self-update hash check against the file server](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt8490482e57c3a5da/6a7c957e33fa8a48b61fcda1/image6.png \"Self-update hash check against the file server\")  \n\nWhen a hash mismatch is detected, the agent downloads the update payload from `documents.ef971a42.workers[.]dev/api/update` as an MSI to `%TEMP%\\update_{8hexchars}.msi`, authenticated with Bearer token `b7ba9e80-0d04-4d9e-b217-c8b3cce335a2`. \n\n![Download updated payload](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt72930667e4f56fb3/6a7c9581bdcff0f7eec3d4c7/image3.png \"Download updated payload\")  \n\nThe download is validated against a 100KB minimum size as a sanity check. The agent then writes a self-deleting batch script to `%TEMP%` that polls the tasklist until the current process exits, executes `msiexec /i /qn REINSTALLMODE=amus` for silent installation, and deletes itself. The batch file is launched via a hidden `cmd.exe` process before the agent terminates, handing off execution to the updated payload.\n\n## Browser URL Monitor and C2 Session Initialization\n\nEvery second, the malware agent calls a browser URL monitor function that reads the foreground browser's address bar via [UI Automation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/framework/ui-automation/ui-automation-overview). It calls `GetForegroundWindow`, resolves the owning process, checks the process name against Chrome, Firefox, Microsoft Edge, Brave, Opera, and Vivaldi, then uses `AutomationElement.FromHandle -\u003e FindFirst(Descendants, ControlType.Edit) -\u003e ValuePattern.Current.Value` to extract the URL, similar to this Stack Overflow [implementation](https://stackoverflow.com/questions/5317642/retrieve-current-url-from-c-sharp-windows-forms-application/5318791#5318791).\n\n![Browser URL monitor via UI Automation](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt6a4c282e76b3193d/6a7c958496b5a66e668759ab/image7.png \"Browser URL monitor via UI Automation\")  \n\nThe extracted URL is matched against a fixed list of targeted banks embedded in the binary, encoded via XOR with a 16-byte key and base64 encoding.\n\n![Encrypted bank/fintech/crypto domains](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt8027bb99057bfbf7/6a7c9586c33f4f25ecd54ea0/image31.png \"Encrypted bank/fintech/crypto domains\")  \n\nThis [GitHub Gist](https://gist.github.com/jiayuchann/e298effb68bd472c9e577a630d0ceb20) contains a list of 59 targeted domains, including Brazilian banking, fintech platforms, and cryptocurrency exchanges, grouped by the target IDs appended to each decrypted domain.\n\nWhen a match hits, the domain target ID is passed to the next state, which initializes the official C2 communication by establishing a WebSocket connection to `wss://mxtestacionamentos[.]com/ws`. The `OnConnect` handler fires, sending a registration packet containing the agent ID (a random GUID at runtime), MachineName, UserName, machine info, timestamp, domain target ID (so the C2 knows which website the victim opened), and a signature. \n\nTo produce a handshake signature, HMAC-SHA256 is used to sign the victim identifier (agent ID, MachineName, UserName, OSVersion, timestamp) using the campaign GUID `70e4f943-e323-4484-97d7-35401bf6812c` as the key.\n\n![Signature generation for the session initialization handshake](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt56fd68ace61939be/6a7c9589c33f4fe8f0d54ea4/image27.png \"Signature generation for the session initialization handshake\")  \n\nThe server then responds with a registration acknowledgment, officially starting the session, and enters the command dispatch loop. At session start, a Task Manager killer is fired every 500ms to prevent the victim from inspecting or terminating the agent's process. \n\n![Killing Task Manager](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt3b3562657d3ace1e/6a7c958c7cfd7add49315108/image24.png \"Killing Task Manager\")  \n\n## C2 Command Table\n\nA capability summarization is described through the opcode table below:\n\n| Opcode | Purpose |\n| :---- | :---- |\n| 2 | Registration ACK, start the Task Manager killer |\n| 4 | Graceful WebSocket disconnect |\n| 5 | Suicide: Kill all sibling processes and exit |\n| 6 | Forced reboot (`shutdown.exe /r /t 0 /f`) |\n| 7 | Suicide-then-Uninstall: kill all processes with the same host binary name except itself (siblings) → uninstall → exit |\n| 16 | Screenshot |\n| 17 | Start streaming the screen |\n| 18 | Stop streaming the screen |\n| 19 | Set screen capture quality (1-100) |\n| 20 | Enumerate monitors |\n| 32 | Mouse move (X, Y, MonitorIndex) |\n| 33 | Mouse click through overlay: parse `{X,Y,Button,MonitorIndex}` → translate to absolute desktop coords → find the implant's own overlay window covering that point → punch a 2x2 region hole in the overlay at that pixel → `SetCursorPos` + `SendInput` mouse-down/up (which lands on whatever real desktop content is underneath the overlay). |\n| 34 | Mouse scroll (Delta, `SendInput`) |\n| 35 | Key tap (KeyCode, `SendInput`) |\n| 37 | Key down (KeyCode, `SendInput`) |\n| 38 | Key up (KeyCode, `SendInput`) |\n| 39 | Start keylogger (`WH_KEYBOARD_LL` hook) |\n| 40 | Flush keylogger, exfil to C2 |\n| 41 | Clipboard hijack (`Clipboard.SetText`) |\n| 48 | File system directory listing |\n| 65 | Get running processes information |\n| 67 | Shell command execution (`cmd.exe /c`) |\n| 80 | Enumerate all visible windows |\n| 81 | Window manager: Kill process of a window / minimize window / restore window / bring window to foreground / close window / move window to another monitor |\n| 83 | Show stall overlay: either progress-steps or fake Windows Update screen |\n| 84 | Teardown overlay |\n| 85 | Toggle screen capture immunity. Enables/disables `WDA_EXCLUDEFROMCAPTURE` on all overlay windows to hide them from screen sharing / screenshots. |\n| 86 | Refresh overlay content |\n| 87 | Show cutout overlay: pin external window inside overlay with visible region cutout |\n| 96 | Show credential prompt overlay |\n\n## Social Engineering UI Framework\n\nA more interesting capability of the banking trojan is a [WPF-based](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/desktop/wpf/overview/) full-screen overlay subsystem that orchestrates bank-themed fraud flows during active C2 sessions.\n\n### Overlay Lifecycle\n\nThe overlay manager spawns one full-screen WPF window per monitor. Windows are configured as borderless, topmost, and hidden from the taskbar (`WindowStyle.None`, `Topmost = true`, `ShowInTaskbar = false`), with a custom `Closing` handle that refuses dismissal until an internal flag is flipped by the operator through the overlay teardown command, preventing the windows from being closed. \n\n![Social engineering overlay settings](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt1f4fbb64e61f28a8/6a7c95916c6eac9e16f0e801/image16.png \"Social engineering overlay settings\")  \n\n![Overlay window close prevention](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt49268fe654e5fe9e/6a7c95939f52512199664410/image5.png \"Overlay window close prevention\")  \n\nAt startup, the manager captures a PNG screenshot of every display via `CopyFromScreen` as the overlay backdrop, creating a “frozen desktop” look. Depending on the currently active overlay, the victim perceives their real desktop environment behind it. \n\nA `500ms` timer continuously reapplies `HWND_TOPMOST` via `SetWindowPos` to defeat any window attempting to surface above the overlay. \n\n![Overlay \"always on top\" setting](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt3606883337159dfd/6a7c9596e02fac1bdc5d0951/image14.png \"Overlay \\\"always on top\\\" setting\")  \n\nIn addition, an anti-capture feature calls `SetWindowDisplayAffinity` with `WDA_EXCLUDEFROMCAPTURE`, rendering the overlay invisible to any screen-capturing tools, allowing the operator to see through their own overlay through the screenshot and screenstreaming commands.\n\n![Disable screen capture to detect overlays](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt2662878c7b506031/6a7c9599227b1c3736592b25/image17.png \"Disable screen capture to detect overlays\")  \n\n### Input Blocker\n\nOn the primary monitor, two hooks are installed: `WH_KEYBOARD_LL` and `WH_MOUSE_LL`. Both hooks check their respective injected flags (`LLKHF_INJECTED` for keyboard, `LLMHF_INJECTED` for mouse), allowing input injected via `SendInput` by the operator’s remote commands to pass through untouched. The keyboard hook swallows Tab, Escape, Alt+F4, Win keys, PrintScreen, Ctrl, Alt, and all navigation keys; the mouse hook blocks right-click, middle-click, and scroll, but allows left-click and movement, so the victim can still interact with the overlay prompts.\n\n![Input blocker initialization and hook function for blocking mouse input](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltb7e70d87e1281cf0/6a7c959b227b1c7943592b29/image8.png \"Input blocker initialization and hook function for blocking mouse input\")  \n\n### Social Engineering UI Builders\n\nFive interchangeable content renderers plug into the overlay framework:\n\nCredential Prompt: Supports three input modes, selected based on the operator's request parameters. \n\n* Phone mode applies real-time Brazilian format masking ((##) ####-#### for 10-digit landlines, (##) #####-#### for 11-digit mobiles) with max 11 digits.   \n* Virtual keypad mode renders an on-screen numeric keypad (buttons 0–9 plus \"limpar\"/clear), displaying input as bullet characters to mimic PIN entry.   \n* Default mode accepts plain text with a configurable max length. \n\nAll modes run input through a quality validator that algorithmically rejects same-digit sequences (`000000`) and ascending/descending runs (`123456`, `654321`) to prevent victims from entering throwaway values.\n\nThe submit action fires the captured values to the C2. \n\n![User-entered values for Credential Prompt sent to C2](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt2556bf76c6371584/6a7c959e448e4eebdc5bafe0/image21.png \"User-entered values for Credential Prompt sent to C2\")  \n\nVishing Wait Screen: Triggers after the victim submits their phone number in the credential prompt. Displays \"Estamos entrando em contato\" (\"We are getting in touch\") with a central image “breathing” animation and three dots with staggered opacity animations (`300ms` offset per dot) producing a \"connecting\" visual. The operator or an accomplice can then call the victim's real phone, impersonating bank security staff. \n\n![Displayed text on Vishing Wait Screen](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltf71a8b76e7ba7100/6a7c95a14c4bfb6084cc7c94/image20.png \"Displayed text on Vishing Wait Screen\")  \n\nProgress Steps: A fully operator-templated stall screen displaying a list of fake processing steps with a randomized animation. Step durations are randomized to total approximately 15 minutes. A timer advances through each step sequentially, visually marking completed steps, highlighting the current one, and dimming the remaining ones. When all steps are complete, the sequence resets to an earlier position with new randomized timings and continues.\n\nFake Windows Update: An alternative stall screen mimicking the Windows 10/11 update-restart screen. Renders a solid `#0078D7` (Windows accent blue) background with a five-ellipse spinning indicator arranged in a circle. A percentage readout jumps by a random 25–35% at randomly selected 50–81-second intervals to mimic the irregular progress behavior of real Windows Updates. Default subtitle: \"Trabalhando em atualizacoes\" (\"Working on updates\").\n\n![Windows \"Working on Updates\" stall overlay](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt063ca7be80c0980a/6a7c95a305b7b573f3185e99/image35.png \"Windows \\\"Working on Updates\\\" stall overlay\")  \n\nCutout Overlay: Cuts a rectangular hole in the full-screen overlay, exposing the underlying application window. The operator specifies hole dimensions via opcode 87, in which the overlay manager builds a themed card with a transparent-border placeholder, computes its screen coordinates post-layout, and cuts a matching region hole using `CreateRectRgn + CombineRgn(RGN_DIFF) + SetWindowRgn`. The target window is repositioned underneath the hole. The result is a real application window framed within the overlay, and the victim interacts with the actual application while the surrounding overlay provides deceptive context.\n\n![Window Region hole cutting to support Cutout Overlays](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltf7a28daf42735c2a/6a7c95a6da3d055252634190/image34.png \"Window Region hole cutting to support Cutout Overlays\")  \n\n## Worm module\n\nThe second module invoked by the loader is `Tcl.WppBot`, is designed to propagate spam and phishing messages at scale, to distribute TCLBANKER. Two distinct agent types were recovered from two different loaders and analyzed:\n\n* A WhatsApp worm that hijacks browser sessions  \n* An Outlook email bot that abuses Microsoft Outlook through COM interop\n\n`Tcl.WppBot` is also .NET Reactor-protected with the same version used to protect `Tcl.Agent`, and so we also managed to statically deobfuscate payloads in this stage. \n\nBoth agents share the same C2 backend, authentication credentials, and operational infrastructure. The C2 URL and API key are decrypted at startup using XOR decryption with a hardcoded key. \n\n* C2 URL: `campanha1-api.ef971a42.workers[.]dev` (Cloudflare Workers app)  \n* API key / Bearer token: `0d21613a-2609-45fc-83ff-d0feaa0c891f`\n\n![Encrypted C2 and API key](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt5d718d5843641888/6a7c95a9e88c6550a4005c66/image29.png \"Encrypted C2 and API key\")  \n\nThe C2 serves a single superset campaign configuration object via the `https://campanha1-api.ef971a42.workers[.]dev/api/campaign` endpoint. Each agent variant deserializes the full object but only reads the fields relevant to its channel.\n\nCaptured configuration:\n\n```\nmessage            : Ola tudo bem?\n                     Preciso
